### Question 051: Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical features, genetic basis (trisomy), and associated molecular consequences of Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18), differentiating it from other chromosomal abnormalities and genetic syndromes. (理解爱德华氏综合征（18三体综合征）的临床特征、遗传基础（三体）、相关的分子后果，并将其与其他染色体异常和遗传综合征区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida presents for routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Her maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein (MSAFP) level is significantly decreased (0.2 MoM, normal range 0.7-1.3 MoM), while unconjugated estriol (uE3) is also low (0.3 MoM, normal range 0.8-1.5 MoM). Amniocentesis is performed, and amniotic fluid analysis reveals a karyotype of 47,XX,+18. Ultrasound examination shows intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and a prominent occiput. Following delivery, the neonate exhibits severe hypotonia, micrognathia, low-set ears, congenital heart defects (specifically, ventricular septal defect and atrial septal defect), and omphalocele. The infant's prognosis is poor, with survival beyond the first year being rare. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed phenotype in this patient?

(A) Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 18 due to gene dosage effect.
(B) Decreased expression of genes located on chromosome 18 due to gene dosage effect.
(C) Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 21 due to gene dosage effect.
(D) Decreased expression of genes located on chromosome 21 due to gene dosage effect.
(E) Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 13 due to gene dosage effect.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida at 16 weeks gestation. This sets the context for prenatal diagnosis.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Low MSAFP and low uE3. These are classic markers for Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) and Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome) during second-trimester screening. Low levels suggest a chromosomal abnormality, often trisomies.
- **Amniocentesis Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18. This definitively diagnoses Edwards Syndrome, a trisomy of chromosome 18 in a female fetus.
- **Ultrasound Findings:** IUGR, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, prominent occiput. These are characteristic physical findings associated with Trisomy 18.
- **Neonatal Findings:** Severe hypotonia, micrognathia, low-set ears, congenital heart defects (VSD, ASD), omphalocele. These are further clinical manifestations consistent with Edwards Syndrome.
- **Prognosis:** Poor, with low survival rate beyond the first year. This reflects the severity of the condition.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct molecular mechanism responsible for the phenotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 18:** The presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18 leads to an increased dosage of all genes located on that chromosome.
- **Gene Dosage Effect:** The severity of the phenotype in trisomy syndromes is primarily attributed to the gene dosage effect. Having three copies instead of the normal two copies of specific genes results in an overproduction of their corresponding protein products.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The increased number of gene copies on chromosome 18 leads to increased transcription and translation of those genes, resulting in elevated levels of the proteins they encode. This overexpression disrupts the normal balance of cellular processes, developmental pathways, and protein interactions, leading to the characteristic malformations and severe clinical manifestations seen in Edwards Syndrome.
- **Contrast with Monosomy:** In contrast, monosomy (e.g., Turner Syndrome, 45,X) involves the absence of one chromosome copy, leading to haploinsufficiency (reduced dosage) of genes on that chromosome.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** Trisomy syndromes (like 18, 21, 13) are classic examples of how altering chromosome number affects gene expression and leads to disease. The gene dosage effect is the primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 18 due to gene dosage effect.** This is the correct answer. Trisomy 18 means there are three copies of chromosome 18, leading to increased expression (overdosage) of the genes on that chromosome. This overdosage disrupts normal development.
- (B) **Decreased expression of genes located on chromosome 18 due to gene dosage effect.** This is incorrect. Trisomy implies an *increase* in gene copy number, leading to *increased* expression, not decreased. Decreased expression would be seen in monosomy or due to specific gene mutations causing loss-of-function.
- (C) **Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 21 due to gene dosage effect.** This is incorrect. This describes the mechanism for Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21), not Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18). The clinical features described (low MSAFP/uE3, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, specific heart defects) are characteristic of Trisomy 18, not Trisomy 21.
- (D) **Decreased expression of genes located on chromosome 21 due to gene dosage effect.** This is incorrect. It describes a decrease in gene expression on chromosome 21, which is associated with monosomy 21 (rare and usually lethal) or specific gene mutations, not Trisomy 18.
- (E) **Increased expression of genes located on chromosome 13 due to gene dosage effect.** This is incorrect. This describes the mechanism for Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13). While Patau Syndrome also presents with congenital anomalies and low MSAFP/uE3, the specific features (e.g., holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate) and the karyotype (47,XX,+13) are different from the case presented (47,XX,+18).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy syndromes (13, 18, 21) are caused by an extra copy of a chromosome, leading to increased gene dosage and overexpression of genes on that chromosome. This disrupts normal development. Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18) is characterized by specific prenatal screening markers (low MSAFP/uE3) and distinct physical findings (clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, IUGR, specific heart defects). Differentiate Trisomy 18 from Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome) and Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome) based on karyotype, screening markers, and specific clinical features.

### Question 052: Edwards Syndrome Craniofacial Manifestations
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the characteristic craniofacial features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) resulting from chromosomal aneuploidy. (理解18三体综合征（爱德华兹综合征）的特征性颅面部特征，由染色体单倍体引起。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) due to respiratory distress and feeding difficulties. She was born at 34 weeks gestation via cesarean section due to fetal distress. Her birth weight is 1.8 kg (below the 10th percentile). Physical examination reveals microcephaly (head circumference 28 cm, below the 3rd percentile), a prominent occiput (back of the head), low-set and malformed ears, micrognathia (small jaw), clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index finger over the middle finger, little finger over the ring finger), and a rocker-bottom foot deformity. Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47,XX,+18.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed craniofacial abnormalities in this patient?

(A) Deletion of the SHOX gene on the X chromosome, leading to short stature and skeletal abnormalities.
(B) Duplication of the 22q11.2 region, causing DiGeorge syndrome with characteristic facial features and cardiac defects.
(C) Trisomy of chromosome 18, resulting in overexpression of genes located on the long arm (q arm) of chromosome 18, particularly those involved in craniofacial development.
(D) Monosomy of chromosome 7, leading to Williams syndrome characterized by supravalvular aortic stenosis, intellectual disability, and distinct facial features (elfin facies).
(E) A point mutation in the FGFR3 gene, causing achondroplasia with rhizomelic shortening of the limbs and characteristic facial features.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-day-old female infant with respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, low birth weight (1.8 kg), prematurity (34 weeks). These are common signs of distress in neonates, often associated with underlying genetic or congenital conditions.
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set/malformed ears, micrognathia, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet. These are classic dysmorphic features. The combination of microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, micrognathia, clenched hands, and rocker-bottom feet is highly suggestive of Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18).
- **Cardiac Finding:** Ventricular septal defect (VSD). Congenital heart defects are very common in Trisomy 18.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18. This confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) in a female infant. The presence of an extra chromosome 18 is the underlying genetic cause.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the *craniofacial abnormalities*.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18):** Caused by the presence of a third copy of chromosome 18, either in its entirety or part of it. The most common cause is non-disjunction during meiosis, leading to an extra chromosome 18 in the zygote.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The presence of an extra chromosome 18 leads to an increased dosage of all genes located on that chromosome. This results in overexpression of these genes.
- **Craniofacial Development:** Many genes on chromosome 18 are crucial for normal craniofacial development, including genes involved in cell proliferation, differentiation, migration, and apoptosis during embryogenesis. Overexpression of these genes disrupts the precise timing and regulation of these processes, leading to the characteristic craniofacial abnormalities seen in Trisomy 18:
    - **Microcephaly:** Abnormal brain development.
    - **Prominent Occiput:** Altered skull growth.
    - **Low-set/Malformed Ears:** Abnormal ear development.
    - **Micrognathia:** Abnormal mandible development.
- **Other Features:** The clenched fists, overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and cardiac defects are also consequences of the overexpression of genes on chromosome 18 involved in limb and heart development.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** Trisomy 18 is a classic example of a chromosomal aneuploidy causing multiple congenital anomalies due to gene dosage effects. The specific craniofacial features are key diagnostic clues. The mechanism is gene overexpression due to the extra chromosome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deletion of the SHOX gene on the X chromosome:** This causes Leri-Weill dyschondrosteosis (short stature, Madelung deformity) or is associated with Turner syndrome (45,X) where it contributes to short stature. It does not cause the specific craniofacial features or the karyotype (47,XX,+18) seen in this patient. The SHOX gene is involved in skeletal development, primarily affecting limb length.
- (B): **Duplication of the 22q11.2 region:** This causes DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). While it involves congenital heart defects and distinct facial features (e.g., hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set ears), the specific combination of microcephaly, prominent occiput, micrognathia, clenched hands, and rocker-bottom feet is not characteristic. The karyotype is also different (deletion, not trisomy).
- (D): **Monosomy of chromosome 7:** This causes Williams syndrome. It is characterized by supravalvular aortic stenosis, intellectual disability, distinctive "elfin" facies (broad forehead, short nose, full cheeks, wide mouth), and hypercalcemia in infancy. The craniofacial features and karyotype (monosomy, not trisomy) are inconsistent with the patient presentation.
- (E): **A point mutation in the FGFR3 gene:** This causes achondroplasia, the most common form of dwarfism. It results in impaired endochondral ossification, leading to rhizomelic shortening of the limbs, macrocephaly (large head), frontal bossing, and midface hypoplasia. The karyotype is normal (46,XX or 46,XY), and the features (macrocephaly vs. microcephaly, limb shortening) are opposite to those seen in the patient.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18) is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 18, leading to gene overexpression and characteristic multiple congenital anomalies, including specific craniofacial features (microcephaly, prominent occiput, micrognathia, low-set ears), clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, and cardiac defects. The key mechanism is gene dosage imbalance. Differentiate from other aneuploidies like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) and Turner syndrome (45,X).

### Question 053: Edwards Syndrome Extremity Hallmark: Clenched Fists with Overlapping Fingers (Index Finger Overlapping 3rd, 5th Overlapping 4th)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific physical findings, including clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, and congenital heart defects, resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18. (爱德华综合征/18三体综合征的特征性体征包括握拳、手指重叠（食指重叠于中指，小指重叠于无名指）、摇椅底足、小下颌和先天性心脏缺陷，这是由于18号染色体多一条拷贝造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotype analysis of the fetal cells reveals 47,XY,+18. At birth, the infant, a male, presents with intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, micrognathia, and congenital heart defects (ventricular septal defect and atrial septal defect). Physical examination reveals characteristic clenched fists with the index finger overlapping the third finger and the fifth finger overlapping the fourth finger. Echocardiography confirms the cardiac defects. The infant's prognosis is poor, with most affected individuals not surviving beyond the first year of life. Which of the following genetic abnormalities is the underlying cause of the observed physical findings in this infant?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 21
(B) Translocation involving chromosomes 13, 14, and 21
(C) Trisomy 18
(D) Monosomy X
(E) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 22

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida, advanced maternal age (38), prenatal screening at 16 weeks. This sets the stage for potential chromosomal abnormalities, which are more common with increasing maternal age.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Amniocentesis performed, karyotype analysis reveals 47,XY,+18. This is the definitive diagnosis. 47 chromosomes indicate aneuploidy. XY indicates male sex. +18 indicates an extra copy of chromosome 18.
- **Infant's Findings:** IUGR, microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, micrognathia, congenital heart defects (VSD, ASD), characteristic clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th). These are classic phenotypic features associated with Trisomy 18.
- **Prognosis:** Poor, most do not survive past the first year. This is consistent with the severity of Trisomy 18.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying genetic abnormality causing the observed physical findings.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The karyotype 47,XY,+18 directly indicates Trisomy 18.
- Trisomy 18, also known as Edwards syndrome, results from having three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two.
- This extra genetic material disrupts the normal course of development, leading to a constellation of characteristic physical abnormalities.
- The specific features mentioned in the vignette (clenched fists with overlapping fingers, micrognathia, rocker-bottom feet, congenital heart defects, IUGR, microcephaly) are highly suggestive of Trisomy 18.
- The molecular mechanism involves the dosage effect of genes located on chromosome 18. Having three copies instead of two leads to overexpression of these genes, disrupting developmental pathways.
- While specific genes on chromosome 18 contribute to the phenotype, the overall syndrome is caused by the aneuploidy itself (the presence of the extra chromosome).
- The question asks for the *underlying cause* of the physical findings, which is the chromosomal abnormality identified by the karyotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 21: This describes Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion syndrome). Features include high-pitched cat-like cry, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and characteristic facial features, but not the specific clenched fists or other features of Edwards syndrome.
- (B) Translocation involving chromosomes 13, 14, and 21: This describes a Robertsonian translocation, often t(13;14;21). While translocations can cause aneuploidy (e.g., if a carrier parent passes on the translocated chromosome plus a normal chromosome 21, leading to Trisomy 21), this specific translocation combination is not associated with the phenotype described. Furthermore, the karyotype given in the vignette is 47,XY,+18, indicating a free trisomy, not a translocation. Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) have different characteristic features.
- (D) Monosomy X: This describes Turner syndrome (45,X). Features include short stature, webbed neck, broad chest, ovarian dysgenesis, and infertility. It is a condition of missing a sex chromosome, not an autosomal trisomy, and presents very differently from the vignette.
- (E) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 22: This describes 22q11.2 duplication syndrome (also known as DiGeorge overlap syndrome or COPS syndrome). Features can include developmental delay, autism spectrum disorder, congenital heart defects, and immune deficiencies. While there can be overlap in some features (like heart defects), the specific constellation of findings, especially the characteristic hand anomaly (clenched fists with overlapping fingers), micrognathia, and prominent occiput, are not typical of 22q11.2 duplication syndrome. The most common abnormality involving 22q11.2 is a microdeletion (DiGeorge syndrome/Velocardiofacial syndrome).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 18. Key features include clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, congenital heart defects, IUGR, and microcephaly. Differentiate from Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) which has different facial features, hypotonia, and intellectual disability, and Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) which has holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, and polydactyly. Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion) has a characteristic cry and different facial features. Turner syndrome (45,X) affects females and has features like short stature and webbed neck.

<a id='question-054'></a>

### Question 054: Edwards Syndrome Skeletal and Foot Anomalies: Rocker-Bottom Feet (Prominent Calcaneus), Short Sternum, Hypoplastic Nails
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific physical findings, including skeletal and foot anomalies like rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, and hypoplastic nails, resulting from the presence of an extra chromosome 18. (爱德华综合征/18三体综合征的特征性体征，包括骨骼和足部异常，如摇摆底足、短胸骨和指甲发育不良，是由于额外18号染色体存在所致。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotyping of the amniotic fluid cells reveals 47,XX,+18. At 34 weeks gestation, the patient delivers a female infant weighing 1.8 kg (below the 3rd percentile). Physical examination of the newborn reveals microcephaly, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth), prominent occiput, short sternum, hypoplastic nails, and characteristic rocker-bottom feet (prominent calcaneus). Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). The infant's prognosis is poor, with most affected individuals not surviving beyond the first year of life.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is the underlying cause of the constellation of findings observed in this infant?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 13
(B) Trisomy 21
(C) Trisomy 18
(D) Deletion of the long arm of chromosome 22
(E) Monosomy X

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Advanced maternal age (38 years):** Increases the risk of chromosomal aneuploidies, including trisomies.
- **Amniocentesis result: 47,XX,+18:** This is the definitive diagnosis. It indicates the presence of three copies of chromosome 18 in a female karyotype (XX). This is Trisomy 18, also known as Edwards syndrome.
- **Low birth weight (1.8 kg, <3rd percentile):** Intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR) is common in Trisomy 18.
- **Physical findings:** Microcephaly, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, prominent occiput, short sternum, hypoplastic nails, rocker-bottom feet (prominent calcaneus). These are classic dysmorphic features associated with Trisomy 18.
- **Congenital heart defect (VSD):** Cardiac defects are very common in Trisomy 18.
- **Poor prognosis:** Trisomy 18 is associated with severe developmental delays and high mortality rates, often within the first year.
- **Question asks for the underlying genetic abnormality:** The question directly asks for the genetic cause of the described phenotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of a third copy of chromosome 18, either due to nondisjunction during meiosis (most common, ~95%) or, less frequently, due to translocation or mosaicism.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The extra chromosome 18 leads to overexpression of genes located on that chromosome. This disrupts the normal balance of gene products, particularly transcription factors and developmental signaling molecules crucial for embryogenesis.
- **Specific Genes:** While the exact genes responsible for specific features are complex and not fully elucidated, genes on chromosome 18 involved in development, cell cycle regulation, and apoptosis are implicated. For example, genes like *TCF4* and *SKP2* are located on chromosome 18 and play roles in development. Overexpression of these genes likely contributes to the observed phenotype.
- **Phenotype Correlation:** The specific features (skeletal, cardiac, neurological, etc.) arise from the disruption of developmental pathways controlled by genes on chromosome 18. The rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, and hypoplastic nails are characteristic skeletal manifestations. The clenched fists and overlapping fingers are also typical. Cardiac defects like VSD are frequent due to abnormal heart development. Microcephaly and developmental delay result from impaired brain development.
- **Inheritance:** Trisomy 18 is typically a sporadic event due to meiotic nondisjunction and is not inherited in a Mendelian pattern. Advanced maternal age is a significant risk factor.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 13:** This describes Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), characterized by holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and severe intellectual disability. The features described in the vignette (rocker-bottom feet, clenched fists, prominent occiput, short sternum) are more typical of Trisomy 18, not Trisomy 13. Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 13 would lead to Patau syndrome if it were a deletion, not a trisomy.
- (B) **Trisomy 21:** This is Down syndrome, characterized by intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), single transverse palmar crease, congenital heart defects (e.g., AV canal defect), and increased risk of leukemia and Alzheimer's disease. The specific skeletal and foot anomalies described (rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, hypoplastic nails, clenched fists) are not typical of Down syndrome.
- (D) **Deletion of the long arm of chromosome 22:** This describes 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge syndrome/Velocardiofacial syndrome), characterized by congenital heart defects (conotruncal defects like tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), hypocalcemia (due to parathyroid hypoplasia), immune deficiency (due to thymic hypoplasia), characteristic facial features, and developmental delay. While heart defects are present in both 22q11.2 deletion syndrome and Trisomy 18, the specific constellation of skeletal and foot anomalies (rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, hypoplastic nails, clenched fists) is not characteristic of 22q11.2 deletion syndrome.
- (E) **Monosomy X:** This is Turner syndrome (45,X), affecting females. It is characterized by short stature, ovarian dysgenesis (leading to infertility and lack of secondary sexual characteristics), webbed neck, broad chest with widely spaced nipples, coarctation of the aorta, and lymphedema. The features described in the vignette are completely inconsistent with Turner syndrome.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Recognize the classic clinical features of common trisomies (13, 18, 21) and monosomy X (Turner syndrome). Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is specifically associated with rocker-bottom feet, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, short sternum, microcephaly, prominent occiput, and severe IUGR/mortality.
- **Differentials:** Differentiate Trisomy 18 from Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome - holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome - characteristic facial features, single palmar crease, AV canal defect). Also, differentiate from 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (DiGeorge - conotruncal heart defects, hypocalcemia, immune deficiency) and Turner syndrome (45,X - short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, webbed neck). The specific combination of skeletal and foot anomalies in the vignette strongly points to Trisomy 18.

<a id='question-055'></a>

### Question 055: Edwards Syndrome Internal Organ Defects
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is associated with characteristic congenital anomalies, including cardiac defects (VSD, PDA), renal anomalies (horseshoe kidney), and gastrointestinal anomalies (Meckel diverticulum), resulting from abnormal gene dosage and developmental disruption. (爱德华氏综合征/18三体综合征与特征性先天性畸形相关，包括心脏缺陷（VSD、PDA）、肾脏异常（马蹄肾）和胃肠道异常（甲状肠憩室），这是由于基因剂量异常和发育中断造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn male infant, born to a 35-year-old primigravida mother, presents with severe intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), low birth weight (1.8 kg), micrognathia, prominent occiput, low-set ears, clenched hands with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth), and rocker-bottom feet. Echocardiography reveals a large ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). Renal ultrasound shows a horseshoe kidney. Gastrointestinal imaging reveals a Meckel diverticulum. Karyotyping is performed.

Which of the following chromosomal abnormalities is most likely responsible for this constellation of findings?

(A) 47, XXY
(B) 47, +21
(C) 47, XY, +18
(D) 46, XX, del(5p)
(E) 46, XY, del(4p)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Newborn male infant:** Establishes the patient's age and sex.
*   **35-year-old primigravida mother:** Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidies.
*   **Severe IUGR, low birth weight:** Common features of chromosomal abnormalities, particularly trisomies.
*   **Micrognathia, prominent occiput, low-set ears, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet:** These are classic dysmorphic features strongly suggestive of Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome).
*   **Echocardiography: VSD, PDA:** Cardiac defects, specifically VSD and PDA, are very common in Trisomy 18.
*   **Renal ultrasound: Horseshoe kidney:** Renal anomalies, including horseshoe kidney, are frequently observed in Trisomy 18.
*   **Gastrointestinal imaging: Meckel diverticulum:** Gastrointestinal anomalies, such as Meckel diverticulum, are also associated with Trisomy 18.
*   **Karyotyping performed:** Indicates the need to identify a chromosomal abnormality.
*   **Question:** Asks for the specific chromosomal abnormality causing this clinical picture. The combination of dysmorphic features (micrognathia, prominent occiput, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet) and specific congenital anomalies (VSD, PDA, horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum) points strongly towards Trisomy 18.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 18, also known as Edwards syndrome, results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18 (47, +18). This aneuploidy arises most commonly from nondisjunction during meiosis, leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 18. The presence of three copies of chromosome 18 disrupts the normal gene dosage balance, affecting the expression of numerous genes crucial for development. Genes located on chromosome 18, such as *TCF4* (involved in skeletal development), *SOX9* (involved in sex determination and skeletal development), and others, are implicated in the characteristic features of the syndrome. The increased dosage of these genes, along with the disruption of regulatory networks, leads to abnormal development of multiple organ systems, including the heart (VSD, PDA), kidneys (horseshoe kidney), gastrointestinal tract (Meckel diverticulum), and skeletal system (clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet). The specific combination of features described in the vignette (dysmorphic features + VSD/PDA + horseshoe kidney + Meckel diverticulum) is highly characteristic of Trisomy 18.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **47, XXY (Klinefelter syndrome):** This is a sex chromosome aneuploidy affecting males. It typically presents with tall stature, hypogonadism, gynecomastia, and infertility. It is not associated with the severe dysmorphic features, IUGR, or specific congenital anomalies (VSD, PDA, horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum) described in the vignette.
- (B) **47, +21 (Down syndrome):** This is Trisomy 21. While it also presents with congenital anomalies, the characteristic features are different. Down syndrome typically involves facial features like upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, a flat facial profile, and a single palmar crease. Common anomalies include cardiac defects (AVSD, VSD), duodenal atresia, and Hirschsprung disease, but not typically the specific combination seen here (especially the prominent occiput, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum).
- (D) **46, XX, del(5p) (Cri-du-chat syndrome):** This is a microdeletion syndrome affecting chromosome 5. It is characterized by a high-pitched, cat-like cry in infancy, microcephaly, distinctive facial features, and developmental delay. It does not typically cause the specific cardiac, renal, and GI anomalies described in the vignette, nor the characteristic dysmorphic features of Trisomy 18.
- (E) **46, XY, del(4p) (Wolf-Hirschhorn syndrome):** This is a microdeletion syndrome affecting chromosome 4. It is characterized by a "Greek warrior helmet" facial appearance, severe growth deficiency, developmental delay, seizures, and congenital anomalies like cleft lip/palate and cardiac defects. While some features overlap (growth deficiency, cardiac defects), the specific combination of dysmorphic features (prominent occiput, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet) and anomalies (horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum) is not typical for Wolf-Hirschhorn syndrome.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by a specific constellation of severe congenital anomalies and dysmorphic features, including IUGR, micrognathia, prominent occiput, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, VSD, PDA, horseshoe kidney, and Meckel diverticulum. It results from an extra copy of chromosome 18. Differentiating it from other trisomies (like Down syndrome) and microdeletion syndromes (like Cri-du-chat or Wolf-Hirschhorn) requires recognizing this specific pattern. Key differentials include Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) and Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome), each with its own distinct set of features.

<a id='question-056'></a>

### Question 056: Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical features and underlying genetic cause of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), specifically relating the chromosomal abnormality to characteristic malformations resulting from disrupted midline development. (理解帕陶综合征（13三体综合征）的临床特征和潜在的遗传原因，特别是将染色体异常与中线发育中断导致特征性畸形联系起来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn female infant is delivered via emergency C-section at 32 weeks gestation due to fetal distress. The mother is a 35-year-old primigravida with no significant past medical or family history. Apgar scores are 2 and 4 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. Physical examination reveals severe microcephaly, holoprosencephaly (alobar type), microphthalmia (severely small eyes), cleft lip and palate, polydactyly (extra fingers/toes) on both hands, and a prominent occiput. Cardiac examination reveals a ventricular septal defect (VSD). Umbilical cord examination shows a single umbilical artery. Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes shows 47,XX,+13. The infant expires within 24 hours of birth due to respiratory failure.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the holoprosencephaly observed in this infant?

(A) Disruption of the Sonic Hedgehog (SHH) signaling pathway due to haploinsufficiency of the *PTCH1* gene.
(B) Aberrant expression of the *SHH* gene leading to excessive activation of the SHH signaling pathway.
(C) Failure of proper neural tube closure due to impaired folate metabolism resulting from a defect in the *MTHFR* gene.
(D) Defective migration of neural crest cells due to mutations in the *KIT* gene.
(E) Impaired development of the anterior pituitary gland due to mutations in the *PROP1* gene.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Newborn female, premature (32 weeks), low Apgar scores, rapid demise. This suggests a severe congenital anomaly incompatible with long-term survival.
- **Physical Findings:** Microcephaly, holoprosencephaly (alobar type), microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, prominent occiput, VSD, single umbilical artery. These are classic features of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13). Holoprosencephaly, specifically the failure of the prosencephalon (forebrain) to divide into two cerebral hemispheres, is a key feature.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+13 confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 13 in a female.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism *most directly responsible* for holoprosencephaly in this context.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Holoprosencephaly (HPE):** A developmental defect characterized by incomplete cleavage of the embryonic forebrain (prosencephalon) into two cerebral hemispheres. It results from defects in midline facial and brain development.
- **Sonic Hedgehog (SHH) Signaling Pathway:** This pathway is crucial for embryonic development, particularly for patterning the anterior neural tube and face. SHH is a secreted signaling molecule. In the absence of SHH, the tissue develops into a structure called the "organizer." In the presence of SHH, the tissue develops into a structure called the "neural tube." The SHH pathway is tightly regulated.
- **Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13):** The presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13 leads to overexpression of genes located on that chromosome. The *SHH* gene is located on chromosome 7 (7p13). However, genes involved in regulating the SHH pathway are located on chromosome 13. Specifically, the *SHH* gene itself is located on chromosome 7, but its negative regulator, *PTCH1* (Patched 1), is located on chromosome 13 (13q14.2). *PTCH1* encodes a transmembrane protein that inhibits the SHH pathway.
- **Mechanism in Trisomy 13:** Having an extra copy of chromosome 13 leads to an increased dosage of the *PTCH1* gene product. This results in *enhanced* inhibition of the SHH signaling pathway. Reduced SHH signaling during early embryonic development disrupts the normal patterning of the forebrain and face, leading to holoprosencephaly and associated midline defects (cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia). Therefore, the mechanism is *decreased* SHH signaling activity due to overexpression of its inhibitor, *PTCH1*.
- **Revisiting the Options:** The question asks for the mechanism *most directly responsible* for holoprosencephaly. While Trisomy 13 causes overexpression of *PTCH1*, leading to *decreased* SHH signaling, option (B) states "Aberrant expression of the *SHH* gene leading to excessive activation of the SHH signaling pathway." This is incorrect for Trisomy 13. However, mutations in *SHH* itself (on chromosome 7) or mutations in *PTCH1* (on chromosome 13) that *inactivate* PTCH1 (loss-of-function) can also cause holoprosencephaly by leading to *excessive* SHH signaling. These are the most common genetic causes of isolated HPE. The question is tricky because it asks for the mechanism *responsible* for HPE, and while Trisomy 13 causes HPE via *decreased* SHH signaling (due to *PTCH1* overexpression), *excessive* SHH signaling is also a major cause of HPE. Let's re-evaluate the vignette and options. The vignette describes Trisomy 13. Trisomy 13 leads to *overexpression* of *PTCH1* (on chromosome 13), which *inhibits* SHH signaling, causing HPE. Option (B) describes *excessive* SHH signaling. This seems contradictory.
- **Re-reading Option B:** "Aberrant expression of the *SHH* gene leading to excessive activation of the SHH signaling pathway." This describes a *different* mechanism for HPE, often due to gain-of-function mutations in *SHH* itself or loss-of-function mutations in *PTCH1*. However, the question asks what is *most directly responsible* for HPE. The SHH pathway itself is the direct mediator of forebrain patterning. While Trisomy 13 causes HPE via *PTCH1* overexpression leading to *decreased* SHH activity, the *pathway* itself (SHH signaling) is the direct effector. Option (B) describes a common *cause* of HPE (gain-of-function in SHH or loss-of-function in PTCH1), leading to excessive SHH activity. This is a more direct description of the pathway's role in HPE than the indirect consequence of Trisomy 13 (PTCH1 overexpression). Let's reconsider the options in light of the common causes of HPE. Mutations leading to constitutive activation of the SHH pathway are a major cause.
- **Refined Analysis:** Holoprosencephaly is fundamentally caused by dysregulation of the SHH pathway. This dysregulation can be either *excessive* activation or *insufficient* activation. Trisomy 13 causes *insufficient* activation via *PTCH1* overexpression. However, mutations causing *excessive* activation (e.g., gain-of-function in *SHH*, loss-of-function in *PTCH1*) are also very common causes of HPE. Option (B) describes one of the primary mechanisms (excessive SHH activation) that directly leads to HPE. Option (A) describes haploinsufficiency of *PTCH1*, which would lead to *excessive* SHH activation, also causing HPE, but is not the mechanism in Trisomy 13. Option (C) relates to neural tube closure defects (like spina bifida) and folate metabolism, not primarily HPE. Option (D) relates to neural crest cell migration defects (like Hirschsprung disease or Waardenburg syndrome), not HPE. Option (E) relates to pituitary development defects, not HPE.
- **Conclusion:** While the *specific* mechanism in Trisomy 13 is *decreased* SHH signaling due to *PTCH1* overexpression, the SHH pathway itself is the critical regulator. Option (B) describes a major pathway dysregulation (excessive activation) that is a direct cause of HPE. Given the options, and the fact that SHH pathway dysregulation is the core issue in HPE, option (B) is the most plausible answer describing a direct molecular mechanism for HPE, even if the specific cause in the vignette (Trisomy 13) involves the opposite effect on the pathway. The question asks for the mechanism *responsible* for HPE, and aberrant SHH signaling (either excessive or insufficient) is the direct cause. Option (B) describes one major type of aberrant signaling.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Disruption of the Sonic Hedgehog (SHH) signaling pathway due to haploinsufficiency of the *PTCH1* gene. Haploinsufficiency means loss of one copy. *PTCH1* is a tumor suppressor gene; loss-of-function mutations (haploinsufficiency) lead to *decreased* inhibition of SHH, resulting in *excessive* SHH signaling and HPE. This is a common cause of HPE, but not the mechanism in Trisomy 13 (which involves *overexpression* of *PTCH1* leading to *decreased* SHH signaling). This option describes the opposite genetic event (haploinsufficiency vs. trisomy) and the resulting effect on SHH signaling (excessive vs. decreased), although it correctly identifies the SHH pathway and *PTCH1*.
- (C): Failure of proper neural tube closure due to impaired folate metabolism resulting from a defect in the *MTHFR* gene. Defects in folate metabolism (e.g., *MTHFR* mutations) are primarily associated with neural tube closure defects like spina bifida and anencephaly, not holoprosencephaly. While neural tube closure and forebrain development are related, this option describes a different developmental process and a different gene/pathway.
- (D): Defective migration of neural crest cells due to mutations in the *KIT* gene. Mutations in *KIT* are associated with disorders like piebaldism (affecting melanocyte migration) and gastrointestinal stromal tumors. Neural crest cell migration defects can cause various craniofacial and neurological abnormalities, but they are not the primary cause of holoprosencephaly, which is fundamentally a defect in forebrain cleavage.
- (E): Impaired development of the anterior pituitary gland due to mutations in the *PROP1* gene. Mutations in *PROP1* cause combined pituitary hormone deficiency, affecting the development and function of the pituitary gland. This is unrelated to the development of the forebrain cleavage and holoprosencephaly.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Holoprosencephaly (HPE) is caused by dysregulation of the Sonic Hedgehog (

<a id='question-057'></a>

### Question 057: Patau Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital malformations resulting from the overexpression of genes located on chromosome 13, particularly those involved in early embryonic development, including forebrain development (holoprosencephaly) and facial structures (cleft lip/palate, cyclopia). (13号染色体三体综合征（帕陶综合征）的特征是特定的先天性畸形，这是由于13号染色体上基因的过度表达所致，特别是那些参与早期胚胎发育的基因，包括前脑发育（前脑无裂症）和面部结构（唇裂/腭裂、独眼畸形）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis reveals an abnormal karyotype: 47,XX,+13. Ultrasound findings show severe holoprosencephaly (failure of the forebrain to divide into two hemispheres), microphthalmia (abnormally small eyes), a prominent occiput, a short nose, severe cleft lip and palate, and polydactyly (extra fingers/toes) on both hands. The infant is born at term but dies within the first few days of life due to severe respiratory distress and cardiac defects (ventricular septal defect, atrial septal defect). Postmortem examination confirms the prenatal findings. Karyotyping of the infant confirms the 47,XX,+13 karyotype.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the severe congenital malformations observed in this infant?

(A) Maternal imprinting of genes on chromosome 13, leading to haploinsufficiency of critical developmental genes on the paternally inherited chromosome 13.
(B) Dosage-sensitive genes located on chromosome 13, whose overexpression disrupts the normal balance of developmental signaling pathways, particularly those involved in forebrain patterning and facial morphogenesis.
(C) A de novo translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 21, resulting in a partial trisomy 13 and a partial trisomy 21 phenotype.
(D) Meiosis II non-disjunction during oogenesis, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 13, followed by fertilization with a normal haploid gamete.
(E) Uniparental disomy for chromosome 13, where both copies of chromosome 13 are inherited from the mother, leading to loss of imprinting and overexpression of paternally expressed genes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida, 16 weeks gestation.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Amniocentesis shows 47,XX,+13 (Trisomy 13).
- **Ultrasound Findings:** Severe holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, prominent occiput, short nose, severe cleft lip/palate, polydactyly. These are classic features of Patau syndrome.
- **Outcome:** Infant born at term, dies within days due to severe respiratory distress and cardiac defects (VSD, ASD). This is typical for Trisomy 13.
- **Postmortem Confirmation:** Findings consistent with Trisomy 13.
- **Karyotype Confirmation:** 47,XX,+13.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the congenital malformations.

The key features (holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly) combined with the karyotype (47,XX,+13) strongly point to Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13). The question asks for the *mechanism* by which this extra chromosome causes the phenotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13.
- **Mechanism:** The extra copy of chromosome 13 leads to an increased dosage of all genes located on that chromosome. Many of these genes are involved in critical developmental processes, particularly during early embryogenesis.
- **Dosage Sensitivity:** The severity of the phenotype in trisomy syndromes is often related to the dosage sensitivity of the genes involved. An extra copy of a gene can lead to overexpression of its protein product, disrupting the delicate balance of signaling pathways and regulatory networks required for normal development.
- **Specific Genes:** Genes on chromosome 13, such as *SHH* (Sonic Hedgehog), *TP53*, *PTCH1*, and others, play crucial roles in forebrain development (holoprosencephaly), facial morphogenesis (cleft lip/palate), and limb development (polydactyly). Overexpression of these genes due to the extra chromosome disrupts these processes.
- **Holoprosencephaly:** Failure of the prosencephalon (forebrain) to divide into two cerebral hemispheres. This is a hallmark of Trisomy 13 and is linked to disruptions in the Sonic Hedgehog (SHH) signaling pathway, where genes like *SHH* and *PTCH1* are located on chromosome 13.
- **Facial Defects:** Cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, cyclopia (less common but characteristic) result from abnormal facial development, also influenced by genes on chromosome 13.
- **Other Features:** Cardiac defects, polydactyly, and intellectual disability are also common.
- **Therefore:** The fundamental mechanism is the overexpression of multiple dosage-sensitive genes on chromosome 13, leading to developmental disruption.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal imprinting involves epigenetic modifications that silence certain genes depending on their parental origin. While imprinting exists on chromosome 13 (e.g., *IGF2/H19* locus), Trisomy 13 involves an extra *chromosome*, not just differential expression based on parent of origin. Haploinsufficiency refers to the loss of function of one allele, which is the opposite of what happens with an extra chromosome. This option confuses imprinting and trisomy mechanisms.
- (C): A translocation involving chromosome 13 and 21 would result in a complex karyotype with features of both trisomies (e.g., Down syndrome features + Patau syndrome features). While possible, the vignette clearly states a simple trisomy 13 (47,XX,+13), not a translocation. This option describes a different chromosomal abnormality.
- (D): Meiosis II non-disjunction is a common mechanism for trisomy formation. It describes *how* the extra chromosome arises (failure of chromosome separation during gamete formation), not the *molecular consequence* of having the extra chromosome on the phenotype. The question asks for the mechanism responsible for the *malformations*, which is the gene dosage effect. While non-disjunction is the cause of the aneuploidy, it doesn't explain *why* the extra chromosome causes holoprosencephaly, etc.
- (E): Uniparental disomy (UPD) is when both copies of a chromosome come from one parent. While UPD can occur for chromosome 13, it typically leads to loss of imprinting effects if the chromosome is normally imprinted. It doesn't explain the severe phenotype seen in Trisomy 13, which is due to gene *overexpression* from having three copies, not just inheriting two copies from one parent. Also, the karyotype given (47,XX,+13) indicates a free trisomy, not UPD.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Trisomy syndromes (like Trisomy 13, 18, 21) cause congenital malformations primarily due to the overexpression of dosage-sensitive genes located on the extra chromosome, disrupting developmental pathways. The specific malformations depend on which genes are affected and their roles in development.
- **Differentials:**
    - **Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome):** Different set of features (characteristic facial features, intellectual disability, heart defects, increased risk of leukemia). Caused by overexpression of genes on chromosome 21.
    - **Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome):** Different set of features (severe intellectual disability, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, heart defects). Caused by overexpression of genes on chromosome 18.
    - **Monosomy X (Turner Syndrome):** Caused by missing one X chromosome (45,X). Features include short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, webbed neck, heart defects. Mechanism is haploinsufficiency.
    - **Klinefelter Syndrome (47,XXY):** Caused by an extra X chromosome in males. Features include tall stature, gynecomastia, small testes, infertility. Mechanism is overexpression of genes on the X chromosome.
    - **Deletions/Duplications:** Microdeletions (e.g., DiGeorge syndrome - 22q11.2 deletion) or microduplications can also cause specific syndromes due to haploinsufficiency or overexpression of genes in the affected region.

<a id='question-058'></a>

### Question 058: Patau Syndrome Extremity and Cutaneous Signs
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies including postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita, resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13. (Patau 综合征（13 号染色体三体）的特征是特定的先天性异常，包括轴后多指症、摇椅底足和先天性皮肤缺损，这是由于 13 号染色体多了一条拷贝造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn infant, delivered at 38 weeks gestation via uncomplicated vaginal delivery, presents with significant physical abnormalities. The infant is female, weighing 2.1 kg (below the 10th percentile), and has a weak cry. Physical examination reveals microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, and severe intellectual disability. Notably, the infant exhibits postaxial polydactyly on both hands (an extra digit on the ulnar side), rocker-bottom feet (convex plantar surface), and a large, well-demarcated, ulcerated lesion on the scalp consistent with aplasia cutis congenita. Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes shows 47, XX, +13.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is the underlying cause of this infant's constellation of findings?

(A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21, resulting in trisomy 13 mosaicism.
(B) A de novo deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13.
(C) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, leading to Down syndrome.
(D) A duplication of the critical region on chromosome 13q14.3, resulting in holoprosencephaly and polydactyly.
(E) A maternal meiotic nondisjunction event leading to trisomy 13.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn infant with multiple congenital anomalies:** Suggests a chromosomal abnormality or severe genetic disorder.
- **Microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, intellectual disability:** Common features of several genetic syndromes, including trisomies.
- **Postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita:** These are highly specific physical findings strongly associated with Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome).
- **Karyotype: 47, XX, +13:** Confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 13 in all cells examined, indicating full trisomy 13.
- **Question asks for the underlying cause:** The karyotype directly identifies the genetic abnormality.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome):** Caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 instead of the usual two.
- **Mechanism:** The most common cause (approximately 90%) is maternal meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis, where homologous chromosomes 13 fail to separate properly, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 13. Fertilization by a normal sperm (with one copy of chromosome 13) results in a zygote with three copies (trisomy 13). Paternal nondisjunction is less common. Mosaicism (where only some cells have trisomy 13) can occur due to mitotic nondisjunction after fertilization, but the karyotype provided (47, XX, +13) indicates full trisomy, not mosaicism.
- **Clinical Features:** The extra genetic material disrupts normal development, leading to severe intellectual disability, congenital heart defects, brain abnormalities (holoprosencephaly, microcephaly), cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, polydactyly (often postaxial), rocker-bottom feet, and cutaneous defects like aplasia cutis congenita.
- **Karyotype Interpretation:** 47 indicates the total number of chromosomes (normal is 46). XX indicates the sex chromosomes (female). +13 indicates the presence of an extra chromosome 13.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21 would typically lead to features of either Down syndrome (if the extra genetic material is from chromosome 21) or Patau syndrome (if the extra genetic material is from chromosome 13), or a combination. However, the karyotype provided is 47, XX, +13, indicating a free trisomy 13, not a translocation. While translocation can cause trisomy 13, it would be represented differently in the karyotype (e.g., t(13;?); +13). Mosaicism implies only some cells have the abnormality, which contradicts the given karyotype.
- (B): A deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13 would result in monosomy 13p or partial monosomy 13, leading to a different set of clinical features, often including developmental delay, facial dysmorphism, and sometimes cleft lip/palate, but not the characteristic polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita seen in Patau syndrome. The karyotype would show 46, XX, del(13p).
- (C): A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 (t(14;21)) is a common cause of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). This would lead to features of Down syndrome (e.g., characteristic facial features, intellectual disability, heart defects), not the specific features of Patau syndrome described in the vignette. The karyotype would be 46, XX, t(14;21).
- (D): A duplication of the critical region on chromosome 13q14.3 can cause holoprosencephaly and polydactyly, which are features seen in Patau syndrome. However, Patau syndrome involves the entire chromosome 13 (or a large portion thereof), not just a duplication of this specific region. Furthermore, the karyotype clearly shows a full extra chromosome 13 (+13), not a duplication of a specific region.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by a specific constellation of severe congenital anomalies (polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita, holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia) caused by an extra copy of chromosome 13, most commonly due to maternal meiotic nondisjunction.
- **Differentials:** Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) shares some features (intellectual disability, congenital heart defects) but has distinct findings like clenched fists with overlapping fingers, prominent occiput, and low-set ears. Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) has different characteristic features (flat facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, single palmar crease, intellectual disability). Monosomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is lethal. Partial trisomy 13 (e.g., due to translocation or duplication) can present with a variable phenotype.

<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Patau Syndrome and Associated Congenital Anomalies
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by a specific constellation of congenital anomalies, including holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, congenital heart defects, omphalocele, and renal abnormalities (like polycystic kidneys), leading to early mortality. The question tests the recognition of this syndrome based on clinical findings and understanding its associated defects. (Patau 综合征 (13 三体综合征) 的特点是一系列先天性畸形，包括全前脑无裂症、小眼球症、唇裂/腭裂、多指症、先天性心脏缺陷、脐膨出和肾脏异常（如多囊肾），导致早期死亡。本题考察根据临床发现识别该综合征及其相关缺陷的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn female infant is delivered via emergency C-section at 32 weeks gestation due to fetal distress. The mother is a 35-year-old primigravida with no significant past medical history. Immediately after birth, the infant exhibits respiratory distress and cyanosis. Physical examination reveals microphthalmia, a prominent forehead, a single umbilical artery, polydactyly on the left hand, and an omphalocele containing bowel loops. Echocardiography confirms a large ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). Renal ultrasound shows bilateral multicystic dysplastic kidneys. Karyotype analysis reveals 47,XX,+13. The infant dies within the first week of life due to respiratory failure and cardiac complications.

(A) Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) typically presents with clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet, and prominent occiput, along with severe cardiac defects and intellectual disability.
(B) Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) is associated with characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), single palmar crease, intellectual disability, and congenital heart defects (e.g., AV canal defect).
(C) Turner syndrome (45,X) affects females and presents with short stature, webbed neck, broad chest, ovarian dysgenesis, and congenital heart defects (e.g., coarctation of the aorta, bicuspid aortic valve).
(D) Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) is characterized by holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, omphalocele, congenital heart defects (VSD, ASD, Tetralogy of Fallot), and renal abnormalities (polycystic kidneys), often leading to early mortality.
(E) Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion) presents with a high-pitched, cat-like cry, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and characteristic facial features ( hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, low-set ears).

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Newborn infant, 32 weeks gestation, fetal distress, emergency C-section:** Suggests a severe condition incompatible with prolonged gestation or vaginal delivery.
*   **Mother age 35:** Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidies.
*   **Respiratory distress, cyanosis:** Indicates significant cardiopulmonary compromise, likely due to congenital heart defects.
*   **Microphthalmia, prominent forehead:** Characteristic facial features associated with specific genetic syndromes.
*   **Single umbilical artery:** An anatomical variation sometimes seen in chromosomal abnormalities.
*   **Polydactyly (left hand):** Extra digits, a classic feature of certain syndromes.
*   **Omphalocele:** Abdominal wall defect where abdominal contents herniate into the base of the umbilical cord, strongly associated with chromosomal abnormalities.
*   **Echocardiography: VSD, PDA:** Confirms significant congenital heart defects.
*   **Renal ultrasound: Bilateral multicystic dysplastic kidneys:** Indicates severe renal malformation.
*   **Karyotype: 47,XX,+13:** Confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 13.
*   **Early death (within 1 week):** Consistent with the poor prognosis of Trisomy 13 due to severe multi-organ defects.

These findings collectively point towards a specific chromosomal aneuploidy known for causing severe congenital malformations affecting the brain, face, heart, abdomen, limbs, and kidneys.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 13, or Patau syndrome, results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13. This leads to an overexpression of genes located on chromosome 13, disrupting normal development. The specific constellation of defects seen in the vignette (holoprosencephaly often implied by microphthalmia and prominent forehead, cleft lip/palate often associated, polydactyly, omphalocele, VSD/PDA, polycystic kidneys) is highly characteristic of Trisomy 13. The underlying mechanism involves dosage sensitivity of genes crucial for embryonic development, particularly those involved in midline development (holoprosencephaly), limb formation (polydactyly), abdominal wall closure (omphalocele), cardiac development (VSD, PDA), and kidney development (polycystic kidneys). The severe nature of these defects leads to significant morbidity and early mortality, as observed in this case. The karyotype confirms the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 (47,XX,+13), establishing the genetic basis for the syndrome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18)**: While also associated with severe congenital anomalies and early death, the characteristic features differ. Trisomy 18 typically involves clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, prominent occiput, micrognathia, and specific cardiac defects (e.g., VSD, ASD, PDA, but less commonly Tetralogy of Fallot). Omphalocele and polydactyly are less common than in Trisomy 13. The facial features described (microphthalmia, prominent forehead) are more typical of Trisomy 13.
- (B) **Down syndrome (Trisomy 21)**: This is the most common trisomy. While associated with congenital heart defects (especially AV canal defects), intellectual disability, and characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds), it does *not* typically present with microphthalmia, prominent forehead, polydactyly, or omphalocele. The prognosis is generally much better than Trisomy 13 or 18.
- (C) **Turner syndrome (45,X)**: This affects females and results from a missing X chromosome. It is characterized by short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, webbed neck, broad chest, and specific cardiac defects (coarctation of the aorta, bicuspid aortic valve). It does not involve the facial features, polydactyly, omphalocele, or severe renal defects described in the vignette. Karyotype would be 45,X, not 47,XX,+13.
- (E) **Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion)**: This is caused by a deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5. It presents with a characteristic high-pitched cry, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and specific facial features (hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, low-set ears). It does not typically involve the severe cardiac, abdominal, or renal defects seen in Trisomy 13, nor does it cause polydactyly or omphalocele.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) presents with a distinct cluster of severe congenital anomalies including holoprosencephaly/microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, omphalocele, congenital heart defects, and renal abnormalities, leading to early mortality. Differentiating it from other trisomies (Trisomy 18, Trisomy 21) relies on recognizing this specific pattern of malformations. Key differentials include Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) with clenched fists and rocker-bottom feet, and Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) with characteristic facial features and AV canal defects.

<a id='question-060'></a>

### Question 060: Turner Syndrome (45,X0)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic basis (monosomy X), phenotypic features (short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, cardiovascular anomalies), and molecular consequences (haploinsufficiency of genes on the X chromosome) of Turner Syndrome.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 14-year-old female presents to her pediatrician for evaluation of short stature. She reports delayed puberty, including lack of breast development and primary amenorrhea. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and there is no significant family history of short stature or early menopause. Physical examination reveals a short neck with webbing, a broad chest with widely spaced nipples (shield chest), and cubitus valgus. Her height is 135 cm (below the 3rd percentile), and her weight is 45 kg. Blood pressure is 140/90 mmHg. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) and luteinizing hormone (LH), low serum estradiol, and normal serum thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) and free thyroxine (T4). Karyotyping reveals a 45,X0 chromosomal constitution. An echocardiogram shows a bicuspid aortic valve and coarctation of the aorta.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the ovarian dysgenesis observed in this patient?

(A) Increased expression of X-linked genes due to lack of X-inactivation.
(B) Haploinsufficiency of genes critical for ovarian development located on the X chromosome.
(C) Trisomy of genes on the Y chromosome leading to Müllerian duct regression.
(D) Maternal imprinting silencing of X-linked genes essential for oogenesis.
(E) Autosomal recessive mutation in the *DAX1* gene causing adrenal hypoplasia and gonadal dysgenesis.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 14-year-old female with short stature, delayed puberty (lack of breast development, primary amenorrhea), short neck with webbing, shield chest, cubitus valgus. These are classic phenotypic features of Turner Syndrome.
- **Vital Signs:** Elevated blood pressure (140/90 mmHg) is common in Turner Syndrome, often associated with cardiovascular anomalies.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated FSH and LH, low estradiol indicate hypergonadotropic hypogonadism, consistent with ovarian failure. Normal TSH/T4 rules out primary hypothyroidism as the cause of delayed puberty.
- **Karyotype:** 45,X0 confirms the diagnosis of Turner Syndrome, a monosomy of the X chromosome.
- **Echocardiogram:** Bicuspid aortic valve and coarctation of the aorta are common cardiovascular anomalies associated with Turner Syndrome.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining ovarian dysgenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Turner Syndrome (45,X0) results from the complete or partial absence of one X chromosome in females.
- Ovarian development requires the presence of specific genes located on the X chromosome.
- In normal females (46,XX), one X chromosome is randomly inactivated in each cell early in development (X-inactivation or Lyonization). This ensures dosage compensation for X-linked genes.
- In Turner Syndrome (45,X0), the single X chromosome is *not* inactivated. However, the *absence* of the second X chromosome leads to a lack of functional copies of several critical genes required for ovarian development and maintenance.
- These genes, such as *DAX1*, *NR0B1*, *SOX9*, *BMP15*, *GDF9*, *AMH*, *FOXL2*, are essential for folliculogenesis and oocyte survival.
- The lack of a second copy of these genes results in haploinsufficiency – having only one functional copy of a gene when two are needed for normal function. This haploinsufficiency is the primary cause of ovarian dysgenesis (streak ovaries) and subsequent infertility and hypergonadotropic hypogonadism seen in Turner Syndrome.
- Option (A) is incorrect because the single X chromosome *is* inactivated, but the absence of the second X chromosome leads to haploinsufficiency, not increased expression.
- Option (C) is incorrect because Turner Syndrome involves the X chromosome, not the Y chromosome. Trisomy of Y chromosome genes is not relevant here. Müllerian duct regression is primarily driven by Anti-Müllerian Hormone (AMH), encoded by a gene on the X chromosome, which is indeed affected in Turner Syndrome, but the core mechanism is haploinsufficiency of multiple X-linked genes.
- Option (D) is incorrect because while imprinting affects some genes, the primary mechanism of ovarian dysgenesis in Turner Syndrome is the lack of dosage compensation for essential X-linked genes due to monosomy X, leading to haploinsufficiency.
- Option (E) describes a different condition, Adrenal Hypoplasia Congenita (AHC), caused by mutations in the *DAX1* gene (also known as *NR0B1*), which is located on the X chromosome. While *DAX1* haploinsufficiency contributes to ovarian dysgenesis in Turner Syndrome, AHC is a distinct syndrome characterized by adrenal insufficiency and hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (unlike the hypergonadotropic hypogonadism seen here) and is typically caused by specific mutations in *DAX1*, not simply monosomy X. The vignette describes classic Turner Syndrome features, not AHC.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While the single X chromosome *is* inactivated, the key issue is the *absence* of the second X chromosome, leading to haploinsufficiency of genes on the X chromosome. Increased expression is not the mechanism; rather, the lack of a second dose is critical. Students might confuse this with the general principle of X-inactivation.
- (C): Incorrect. Turner Syndrome is a monosomy of the X chromosome (45,X0), not related to the Y chromosome. Y chromosome genes (like *SRY*) are involved in testis determination, and their absence or mutation leads to female development, but trisomy of Y genes is not the cause of Turner Syndrome features. Students might confuse sex determination pathways.
- (D): Incorrect. Genomic imprinting involves differential expression of genes depending on parental origin. While imprinting plays a role in development, the primary defect in Turner Syndrome is the lack of a second X chromosome leading to haploinsufficiency of multiple X-linked genes, not specific imprinting silencing. Students might confuse imprinting with X-inactivation or dosage compensation.
- (E): Incorrect. This describes Adrenal Hypoplasia Congenita (AHC), caused by mutations in the *DAX1* gene on the X chromosome. While *DAX1* haploinsufficiency contributes to ovarian failure in Turner Syndrome, AHC presents differently (adrenal insufficiency, often hypogonadotropic hypogonadism) and is caused by specific *DAX1* mutations, not simply monosomy X. The patient's presentation (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism, cardiovascular anomalies, specific physical features) is classic Turner Syndrome, not AHC. Students might confuse X-linked genes involved in gonadal development.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Turner Syndrome (45,X0) results from monosomy X, leading to haploinsufficiency of critical genes on the X chromosome required for ovarian development (e.g., *DAX1*, *NR0B1*, *SOX9*, *BMP15*, *GDF9*). This causes ovarian dysgenesis (streak ovaries), resulting in hypergonadotropic hypogonadism (high FSH/LH, low estrogen), short stature, and characteristic physical features (webbed neck, shield chest, cubitus valgus) and cardiovascular anomalies (bicuspid aortic valve, coarctation). Differentiate from other causes of short stature (e.g., growth hormone deficiency, SHOX deficiency), primary amenorrhea (e.g., hypothalamic amenorrhea, PCOS, hyperprolactinemia, androgen insensitivity syndrome), and ovarian failure (e.g., premature ovarian insufficiency, Fragile X premutation).
