<a id='question-071'></a>

### Question 071: Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18 / 47,XX,+18)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical features and genetic basis of Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome), including characteristic physical findings, associated anomalies, and the underlying chromosomal abnormality. (理解18三体综合征（爱德华兹综合征）的临床特征和遗传基础，包括特征性体格发现、相关异常以及潜在的染色体异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida (G1P0) presents at 34 weeks gestation for a routine prenatal ultrasound. The ultrasound reveals intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR) and several fetal anomalies, including a prominent occiput, micrognathia (small jaw), clenched hands with overlapping fingers (index finger over middle finger, fifth finger over fourth finger), rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects (specifically, a ventricular septal defect - VSD). Amniocentesis is performed, and fetal karyotyping reveals a 47,XX,+18 chromosomal constitution. The mother's age is 32, and she has no significant past medical history or family history of genetic disorders. Postnatally, the infant exhibits severe developmental delay, feeding difficulties, and respiratory distress. The infant survives for only 3 weeks before succumbing to complications related to the congenital heart defect and respiratory failure.

Which of the following genetic mechanisms is the MOST likely cause of this infant's condition?

(A) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 18 and 21, leading to an unbalanced translocation resulting in monosomy 18.
(B) A de novo interstitial deletion on the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 18, resulting in partial monosomy 18.
(C) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I, resulting in a gamete with an extra copy of chromosome 18.
(D) A pericentric inversion on chromosome 18, leading to a duplication of the short arm and deletion of the long arm.
(E) A uniparental disomy (UPD) event for chromosome 18, where both copies are inherited from the father.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida at 34 weeks gestation. This age is considered advanced maternal age (AMA), which increases the risk of chromosomal aneuploidies.
*   **Ultrasound Findings:** IUGR, prominent occiput, micrognathia, clenched hands with overlapping fingers (characteristic "index finger over middle finger" and "fifth finger over fourth finger" sign), rocker-bottom feet, congenital heart defects (VSD). These are classic physical findings strongly suggestive of Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome).
*   **Amniocentesis/Karyotyping:** 47,XX,+18. This confirms the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18 in a female fetus. This is the defining genetic feature of Trisomy 18.
*   **Postnatal Course:** Severe developmental delay, feeding difficulties, respiratory distress, early mortality (3 weeks). This clinical course is typical for infants born with Trisomy 18, reflecting the severe systemic effects of the extra chromosome.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely genetic mechanism causing the condition (Trisomy 18).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two. The karyotype is 47,XX,+18 (female) or 47,XY,+18 (male).
*   **Mechanism:** The most common cause (approximately 95% of cases) is nondisjunction during gametogenesis (meiosis I or meiosis II) in either the mother or the father. Nondisjunction leads to the formation of a gamete (egg or sperm) with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1). Fertilization of this gamete by a normal gamete (n) results in a zygote with three copies of chromosome 18 (2n+1).
*   **Maternal Age Effect:** The risk of nondisjunction increases significantly with advanced maternal age (typically >35 years). While the mother in this vignette is 32, the risk is still elevated compared to younger women.
*   **Clinical Features:** The clinical features described (IUGR, characteristic hand/foot deformities, micrognathia, prominent occiput, heart defects, severe developmental delay, early mortality) are pathognomonic for Trisomy 18. These features arise from the dosage effect of the extra genes on chromosome 18, disrupting normal development.
*   **Option C Explanation:** Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I is the most frequent cause of Trisomy 18. During meiosis I, homologous chromosomes fail to separate properly, leading to a gamete with both copies of chromosome 18. Fertilization results in 47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18. This mechanism directly explains the observed karyotype and clinical presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 18 and 21:** Robertsonian translocations involve the fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22) at the centromere. While a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 21 can cause Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), a translocation involving chromosome 18 is much rarer. Furthermore, a translocation typically results in an *unbalanced* karyotype (e.g., monosomy 18 and duplication of another chromosome) if inherited from a carrier parent, or a *balanced* karyotype if the parent is a carrier and the child inherits the balanced translocation. The vignette describes a *trisomy* 18 (47,XX,+18), not monosomy 18 or a balanced translocation. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
*   **(B) De novo interstitial deletion on the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 18:** An interstitial deletion results in a *monosomy* for the deleted region, not a *trisomy* for the entire chromosome. A deletion on the p arm would lead to a partial monosomy 18 syndrome (e.g., 18p- syndrome), which has a different clinical phenotype (e.g., severe intellectual disability, growth retardation, characteristic facial features, but not the specific features of Trisomy 18 like clenched hands and rocker-bottom feet). The karyotype would show a deletion, not an extra chromosome. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
*   **(D) Pericentric inversion on chromosome 18:** A pericentric inversion involves a segment of a chromosome breaking in two places and reinserting in an inverted order. This typically does not change the amount of genetic material but can lead to problems during meiosis (e.g., formation of inviable gametes or offspring with unbalanced chromosome complements). It does not directly cause a trisomy for the entire chromosome 18. While inversions can sometimes lead to duplications/deletions, the primary mechanism for Trisomy 18 is nondisjunction. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
*   **(E) Uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 18:** UPD occurs when an individual inherits both copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copy from the other parent. While UPD can cause certain genetic disorders (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndrome), it does not result in a *trisomy*. In UPD for chromosome 18, the individual would have two copies of chromosome 18, one from each parent (or both from one parent), resulting in a normal diploid state (46,XX or 46,XY) or potentially issues related to imprinting if applicable, but not the extra chromosome characteristic of Trisomy 18. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is most commonly caused by nondisjunction during meiosis, leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18. Key clinical features include IUGR, micrognathia, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. Differentiate Trisomy 18 from Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome - holoprosencephaly, polydactyly) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome - characteristic facial features, intellectual disability, heart defects). Also, distinguish aneuploidy (like trisomy) from chromosomal structural rearrangements (translocations, deletions, inversions) and imprinting disorders (like UPD).

### Question 072: Edwards Syndrome Craniofacial Manifestations
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of characteristic craniofacial features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome), a chromosomal abnormality resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18. (本题考察的是18三体综合征（爱德华兹综合征）相关的特征性颅面部表现，这是一种由18号染色体额外拷贝引起的染色体异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) due to respiratory distress and feeding difficulties. She was born at 34 weeks gestation via cesarean section to a 32-year-old primigravida mother. The mother reports no significant past medical history or family history of genetic disorders. Physical examination reveals a low birth weight (1800g), microcephaly (head circumference 28 cm, <3rd percentile), prominent occiput, low-set and malformed ears, micrognathia (small jaw), clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index finger over middle finger, little finger over ring finger), and a rocker-bottom foot deformity. Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47,XX,+18.

Which of the following sets of findings is MOST characteristic of the craniofacial features observed in this infant?

(A) Frontal bossing, hypertelorism, upslanting palpebral fissures, single transverse palmar crease.
(B) Microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set malformed ears, micrognathia.
(C) Macrocephaly, brachycephaly, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge.
(D) Micrognathia, hypertelorism, downslanting palpebral fissures, low-set ears.
(E) Macrocephaly, prominent occiput, upslanting palpebral fissures, single transverse palmar crease.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-day-old female infant with respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, born prematurely (34 weeks).
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Low birth weight, microcephaly (small head), prominent occiput (back of head), low-set and malformed ears, micrognathia (small jaw), clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet. These are classic dysmorphic features.
- **Cardiac Finding:** Ventricular septal defect (VSD) is common in chromosomal abnormalities.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18 confirms Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic craniofacial features observed in this infant, linking the clinical findings to the specific syndrome.

The key craniofacial features described in the vignette (microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set/malformed ears, micrognathia) are hallmarks of Trisomy 18. The question asks to identify the option that lists these specific features.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is caused by the presence of a third copy of chromosome 18. This extra genetic material disrupts the normal development of multiple organ systems, leading to severe intellectual disability, growth retardation, and characteristic physical anomalies. The craniofacial features are particularly distinctive and include:
- **Microcephaly:** Abnormally small head size.
- **Prominent Occiput:** The back of the head appears unusually large or prominent relative to the overall head size.
- **Low-set, Malformed Ears:** Ears are positioned lower than normal and may have an abnormal shape.
- **Micrognathia:** Small or underdeveloped jaw.
Other common features include clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over middle, little over ring), rocker-bottom feet, congenital heart defects (like VSD), and severe developmental delay. The underlying mechanism is gene dosage imbalance due to the extra chromosome 18, affecting the expression of numerous genes critical for development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Frontal bossing, hypertelorism (widely spaced eyes), upslanting palpebral fissures (eyes slant upwards), single transverse palmar crease. These features are characteristic of Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome), not Trisomy 18. Frontal bossing is a prominent forehead, hypertelorism is wide-set eyes, and upslanting palpebral fissures are typical eye findings in Down Syndrome. A single transverse palmar crease (Simian crease) is also common in Down Syndrome.
- (C): Macrocephaly (large head), brachycephaly (short, broad head), epicanthal folds (skin folds covering the inner corner of the eye), flat nasal bridge. Macrocephaly is the opposite of the microcephaly seen in Trisomy 18. Brachycephaly, epicanthal folds, and a flat nasal bridge are features often associated with Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21) or other syndromes, but not typically Trisomy 18.
- (D): Micrognathia, hypertelorism, downslanting palpebral fissures (eyes slant downwards), low-set ears. While micrognathia and low-set ears are features of Trisomy 18, hypertelorism and downslanting palpebral fissures are not characteristic. Downslanting palpebral fissures are more commonly seen in Trisomy 21 or other syndromes like Noonan syndrome. Hypertelorism is wide-set eyes, which is not typical for Trisomy 18.
- (E): Macrocephaly, prominent occiput, upslanting palpebral fissures, single transverse palmar crease. Macrocephaly is incorrect for Trisomy 18. Upslanting palpebral fissures and a single transverse palmar crease are features of Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome). While a prominent occiput can be seen in Trisomy 18, the combination with macrocephaly and features of Down Syndrome makes this option incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing the specific constellation of craniofacial features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome): microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set/malformed ears, and micrognathia. Differentiating Trisomy 18 from Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome) is crucial, as they have distinct features (e.g., microcephaly vs. macrocephaly/brachycephaly, prominent occiput vs. flat occiput, micrognathia vs. normal jaw, clenched fists/rocker-bottom feet vs. single palmar crease/sandal gap). (本题的关键在于区分18三体综合征（爱德华兹综合征）和21三体综合征（唐氏综合征）的特征性颅面部表现。例如，18三体综合征表现为小头畸形、枕骨突出、低位畸形耳、小下颌；而21三体综合征表现为大头畸形、短宽头、上斜眼裂、单横掌纹等。)

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Edwards Syndrome Extremity Hallmark: Clenched Fists with Overlapping Fingers (Index Finger Overlapping 3rd, 5th Overlapping 4th) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific physical findings, including clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, low-set ears, and congenital heart defects. The underlying cause is the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18. (爱德华综合征/18三体综合征的特征包括握拳、手指重叠（食指重叠于中指，小指重叠于无名指）、摇椅底足、小下颌、低位耳朵和先天性心脏缺陷。根本原因是18号染色体多一条拷贝。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotype analysis of the amniotic fluid cells reveals 47,XX,+18. At 34 weeks gestation, she delivers a female infant weighing 2.1 kg (10th percentile). Physical examination of the newborn reveals micrognathia, low-set ears with prominent occiput, bilateral rocker-bottom feet, and clenched fists with the index finger overlapping the third finger and the fifth finger overlapping the fourth finger. Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). The infant develops respiratory distress shortly after birth and dies within the first week of life despite intensive care. Postmortem examination confirms the clinical findings and the karyotype. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is the MOST likely cause of the characteristic clenched fist deformity observed in this infant?

(A) Aberrant expression of HOX genes on chromosome 18 leading to altered limb bud development.
(B) Increased expression of fibroblast growth factor receptor 3 (FGFR3) due to the extra copy of chromosome 18, causing premature fusion of phalanges.
(C) Dosage-sensitive genes on chromosome 18, particularly those involved in skeletal development and muscle tone regulation, leading to abnormal hand positioning and contractures.
(D) Disruption of the Wnt signaling pathway due to the extra copy of chromosome 18, resulting in abnormal digit formation and flexion contractures.
(E) Increased expression of sonic hedgehog (SHH) signaling molecules from the extra chromosome 18 copy, causing polydactyly and syndactyly.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida, advanced maternal age (38), delivers a female infant at 34 weeks.
- **Prenatal Diagnosis:** Amniocentesis reveals karyotype 47,XX,+18 (Trisomy 18 / Edwards Syndrome).
- **Infant Findings:** Low birth weight (2.1 kg, 10th percentile), micrognathia, low-set ears, rocker-bottom feet, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), VSD.
- **Outcome:** Respiratory distress, death within the first week.
- **Question:** Mechanism causing the characteristic clenched fist deformity.
- **Key Clue:** The specific physical findings (clenched fists, overlapping fingers, micrognathia, rocker-bottom feet, VSD) combined with the confirmed karyotype (Trisomy 18) point directly to Edwards syndrome. The question asks for the molecular basis of the *clenched fist* deformity.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18):** Caused by an extra copy of chromosome 18. This leads to overexpression of genes located on chromosome 18.
- **Clenched Fist Deformity:** A hallmark feature of Trisomy 18. It involves the hands being held in a flexed position, with the index finger overlapping the middle finger and the little finger overlapping the ring finger. This is thought to be due to a combination of factors related to the extra genetic material.
- **Mechanism:** The extra chromosome 18 contains multiple genes, some of which are dosage-sensitive and play critical roles in development, particularly skeletal development, muscle tone, and central nervous system function. Overexpression of these genes disrupts normal development. Specifically, genes involved in skeletal patterning, muscle development, and potentially neuronal control of muscle tone are implicated. The clenched fist is likely a consequence of abnormal muscle tone (hypertonia in the upper limbs) and potentially altered skeletal development, leading to fixed flexion contractures. While specific genes like HOX, FGFR3, Wnt pathway components, and SHH are involved in limb development, the clenched fist in Trisomy 18 is generally attributed to the overall disruption caused by the overexpression of *multiple* dosage-sensitive genes on chromosome 18 affecting skeletal and muscular systems, rather than a single specific pathway disruption like those listed in the distractors. Option (C) best captures this multifactorial, dosage-dependent effect of the extra chromosome 18 on genes crucial for limb development and muscle tone.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): HOX genes are crucial for anterior-posterior limb patterning. While mutations in HOX genes can cause limb malformations (e.g., hand-foot-genital syndrome), the specific clenched fist deformity of Trisomy 18 is not primarily attributed to aberrant HOX gene expression, although HOX genes are located on chromosome 18. The phenotype is more complex than just patterning defects.
- (B): Increased FGFR3 expression is associated with achondroplasia and other skeletal dysplasias, often causing rhizomelic shortening (proximal limb shortening) and craniosynostosis. While FGFR3 is on chromosome 18, its overexpression doesn't typically cause the specific clenched fist deformity seen in Trisomy 18. Trisomy 18 involves multiple genes, not just FGFR3 overexpression leading to premature fusion.
- (D): The Wnt signaling pathway is essential for limb bud formation and digit patterning. Disruptions can lead to various limb defects, including polydactyly or syndactyly. While Wnt pathway genes are on chromosome 18, the clenched fist is more related to muscle tone and contractures than primary digit patterning defects caused by Wnt disruption.
- (E): Sonic hedgehog (SHH) signaling is critical for ventral limb patterning and digit formation. Aberrant SHH signaling is associated with conditions like holoprosencephaly and polydactyly. While SHH is on chromosome 18, its overexpression is not the primary cause of the clenched fist deformity in Trisomy 18. The clenched fist involves flexion contractures, not primarily digit number or fusion abnormalities.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) presents with characteristic features including clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, and congenital heart defects. The underlying cause is the presence of an extra chromosome 18, leading to overexpression of multiple dosage-sensitive genes affecting development. The clenched fist is likely due to a combination of abnormal muscle tone and skeletal development resulting from this genetic imbalance. Differential diagnoses for limb abnormalities include other trisomies (e.g., Trisomy 13 - Patau syndrome, Trisomy 21 - Down syndrome), specific gene mutations affecting limb development (e.g., Holt-Oram syndrome, Fanconi anemia), and teratogen exposure.

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Edwards Syndrome Skeletal and Foot Anomalies: Rocker-Bottom Feet (Prominent Calcaneus), Short Sternum, Hypoplastic Nails - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies, including skeletal and foot deformities like rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, and hypoplastic nails, resulting from abnormal gene dosage of genes located on chromosome 18 during development. (爱德华综合征/18三体综合征的特征性先天性畸形，包括骨骼和足部畸形，如摇篮底足、短胸骨和指甲发育不良，是由于发育过程中18号染色体上基因剂量异常造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) due to respiratory distress and feeding difficulties. She was born at 34 weeks gestation via cesarean section due to fetal distress. Her birth weight was 1.8 kg (below the 10th percentile). Physical examination reveals microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth), and bilateral rocker-bottom feet with a prominent calcaneus. Chest X-ray shows a short sternum and bell-shaped thorax. Nails are hypoplastic. Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47,XX,+18.

(A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21.
(B) A deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5.
(C) A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13.
(D) A maternal meiotic nondisjunction event leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18.
(E) A paternal meiotic nondisjunction event leading to an extra copy of chromosome 13.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-day-old female infant, premature (34 weeks), low birth weight (1.8 kg, <10th percentile), respiratory distress, feeding difficulties. These suggest a significant congenital syndrome.
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (characteristic hand posture), bilateral rocker-bottom feet (prominent calcaneus), short sternum, bell-shaped thorax, hypoplastic nails. These are classic features strongly suggestive of Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome).
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18 confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 18 in a female infant.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying genetic mechanism causing this condition.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two.
- **Clinical Features:** The described features (rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, clenched hands, microcephaly, low-set ears, etc.) are highly characteristic of Trisomy 18. These arise due to the abnormal dosage of multiple genes on chromosome 18, which are crucial for normal development, particularly skeletal, cardiac, and central nervous system development.
- **Mechanism:** The most common cause of Trisomy 18 is nondisjunction during meiosis, either maternal or paternal. Nondisjunction is the failure of homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) to separate properly. This results in a gamete (egg or sperm) with an extra copy of chromosome 18. If this gamete is fertilized by a normal gamete, the resulting zygote will have three copies of chromosome 18.
- **Karyotype Interpretation:** 47,XX,+18 indicates a total of 47 chromosomes, with the female sex chromosome complement (XX) and an extra chromosome 18.
- **Maternal vs. Paternal Nondisjunction:** Nondisjunction can occur during oogenesis (maternal) or spermatogenesis (paternal). Maternal nondisjunction is more common for Trisomy 18 (and Trisomy 21). The age of the mother is a significant risk factor for nondisjunction events.
- **Option D:** Correctly identifies maternal meiotic nondisjunction as a common cause leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18, consistent with the karyotype and clinical presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21 would lead to Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) or Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) features, or potentially a balanced translocation carrier state, not the specific features of Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18). The clinical features described (rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, clenched fists) are not typical for Trisomy 13 or 21.
- (B): A deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p-) causes Cri-du-chat syndrome. This syndrome is characterized by a high-pitched, cat-like cry in infancy, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and distinctive facial features, but not the specific skeletal and foot anomalies seen in Edwards syndrome.
- (C): A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13 would lead to features of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), such as holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and severe intellectual disability. The clinical presentation in the vignette is inconsistent with Trisomy 13.
- (E): A paternal meiotic nondisjunction event leading to an extra copy of chromosome 13 would result in Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), characterized by different clinical features than those described (e.g., holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly). While nondisjunction is a mechanism, it involves the wrong chromosome (13 instead of 18).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies including rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, clenched fists, microcephaly, and low-set ears. It is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction, often maternal, leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18. Key differentials include Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) and Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), which have distinct clinical features and chromosomal abnormalities. Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion) and other chromosomal abnormalities must also be considered in the differential diagnosis of infants with multiple congenital anomalies.

<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Edwards Syndrome Internal Organ Defects: Ventricular Septal Defect (VSD), Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA), Horseshoe Kidney, Meckel Diverticulum - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is associated with characteristic congenital anomalies, including cardiac defects (VSD, PDA), renal anomalies (horseshoe kidney), and gastrointestinal anomalies (Meckel diverticulum), resulting from abnormal gene dosage and developmental disruption.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old female infant presents to the pediatric cardiology clinic with failure to thrive, cyanosis, and recurrent respiratory infections. Her mother reports that she was born prematurely at 32 weeks gestation. Physical examination reveals a prominent occiput, micrognathia, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, and a rocker-bottom foot. Auscultation reveals a harsh systolic murmur and a continuous "machinery" murmur. An echocardiogram confirms a large ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). A renal ultrasound shows a horseshoe kidney. A gastrointestinal series reveals a Meckel diverticulum. Karyotyping performed on the infant reveals 47,XX,+18.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the constellation of congenital anomalies observed in this patient?

(A) Imprinting defect affecting genes on chromosome 18, leading to overexpression of maternal alleles.
(B) Meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis resulting in an extra copy of chromosome 18 in the zygote.
(C) Uniparental disomy for chromosome 18, leading to loss of heterozygosity and expression of only one parental allele set.
(D) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 18, resulting in duplication of critical gene regions.
(E) Mosaicism for trisomy 18, where only some cells have the extra chromosome, leading to variable phenotypic expression.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-month-old female with failure to thrive, cyanosis, recurrent respiratory infections. These symptoms suggest significant congenital heart defects.
- **Physical Exam:** Prominent occiput, micrognathia, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom foot. These are classic dysmorphic features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome).
- **Cardiac Findings:** Harsh systolic murmur (likely VSD) and continuous "machinery" murmur (PDA) confirmed by echocardiogram. VSD and PDA are common cardiac defects in Trisomy 18.
- **Renal Finding:** Horseshoe kidney identified by renal ultrasound. Renal anomalies are frequently seen in Trisomy 18.
- **Gastrointestinal Finding:** Meckel diverticulum identified by GI series. GI anomalies are also associated with Trisomy 18.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18 confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) in a female.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the constellation of anomalies.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is a chromosomal abnormality characterized by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18.
- The most common cause of Trisomy 18 is **meiotic nondisjunction**, an error in cell division during the formation of egg or sperm cells (gametogenesis).
- **Nondisjunction** occurs when homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) fail to separate properly.
- If nondisjunction occurs during **oogenesis** (egg formation), the resulting egg cell will have either 24 chromosomes (nullisomy) or 25 chromosomes (trisomy).
- If this egg is fertilized by a normal sperm (23 chromosomes), the resulting zygote will have either 47 chromosomes (monosomy 18) or 47 chromosomes (trisomy 18).
- The presence of an extra chromosome 18 leads to an **aneuploidy**, specifically a trisomy.
- The extra genetic material disrupts the normal balance of gene expression (gene dosage effect) and interferes with the complex regulatory networks controlling embryonic development.
- This disruption affects the development of multiple organ systems, leading to the characteristic congenital anomalies seen in Trisomy 18, including cardiac defects (VSD, PDA), renal anomalies (horseshoe kidney), gastrointestinal anomalies (Meckel diverticulum), and the specific dysmorphic features observed in the patient.
- Therefore, meiotic nondisjunction is the primary molecular mechanism causing the extra chromosome 18 and subsequent phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Imprinting defect affecting genes on chromosome 18, leading to overexpression of maternal alleles.** Imprinting involves differential expression of genes depending on parental origin. While imprinting defects can cause syndromes (e.g., Prader-Willi, Angelman), Trisomy 18 is caused by having three copies of the *entire* chromosome, not a specific imprinting issue. The phenotype is due to overall gene dosage imbalance, not allele-specific expression.
- (C): **Uniparental disomy for chromosome 18, leading to loss of heterozygosity and expression of only one parental allele set.** Uniparental disomy (UPD) occurs when both copies of a chromosome come from the same parent. While UPD can cause disease (e.g., if it involves an imprinted chromosome or reveals recessive disorders), it results in two copies from one parent and zero from the other, not three copies total. Trisomy 18 involves having three copies of chromosome 18, usually from different parents (e.g., two from mother, one from father, or vice versa).
- (D): **Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 18, resulting in duplication of critical gene regions.** Robertsonian translocations involve fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (like 18). While a Robertsonian translocation *involving* chromosome 18 can lead to Trisomy 18 (if an unbalanced gamete is produced), the *most common* cause is nondisjunction. Furthermore, translocation carriers often have a normal phenotype or milder features due to the translocation chromosome being structurally normal, whereas Trisomy 18 due to nondisjunction involves three copies of the *entire* chromosome 18, leading to a more severe phenotype. The question asks for the *best* explanation for the constellation of anomalies, and nondisjunction is the overwhelmingly most frequent cause.
- (E): **Mosaicism for trisomy 18, where only some cells have the extra chromosome, leading to variable phenotypic expression.** Mosaic Trisomy 18 occurs when nondisjunction happens *after* fertilization (mitotic nondisjunction) or during early embryonic development. This results in a mixture of cells: some with 46 chromosomes and some with 47 chromosomes (Trisomy 18). Mosaicism typically leads to a milder phenotype than full Trisomy 18 because not all cells are affected. The patient described has classic, severe features of Trisomy 18, making full trisomy due to nondisjunction more likely than mosaicism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18. This results in a characteristic phenotype including severe congenital anomalies. Key differentials for aneuploidy include Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome, most common cause is nondisjunction) and Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome, most common cause is nondisjunction). Other causes of aneuploidy include translocation and mosaicism, but nondisjunction is the most frequent mechanism.

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13 / 47,XX,+13)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by severe congenital malformations, including holoprosencephaly, microcephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects, resulting from an extra copy of chromosome 13. The clinical presentation and specific malformations are key diagnostic features. (Patau 综合征 (13 三体综合征) 的特点是严重的先天性畸形，包括全前脑发育不全、小头畸形、唇裂/腭裂、多指症和心脏缺陷，这是由于 13 号染色体多了一条拷贝造成的。临床表现和特定的畸形是关键的诊断特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotyping of the amniotic fluid cells reveals a female fetus with a 47,XX,+13 karyotype. Ultrasound findings show severe microcephaly, holoprosencephaly (alobar type), a single umbilical artery, polydactyly on the left hand, and a ventricular septal defect (VSD). The parents are counseled regarding the prognosis, which is typically poor, with most affected infants dying within the first year of life due to severe neurological impairment and associated congenital anomalies. The parents decide to continue the pregnancy. At birth, the infant presents with a prominent forehead, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, and postaxial polydactyly (extra digit on the ulnar side of the hand). Echocardiography confirms the VSD. The infant's neurological examination reveals severe hypotonia and absent deep tendon reflexes. The infant dies at 6 months of age due to respiratory failure secondary to severe central apnea. Which of the following genetic abnormalities is most consistent with this clinical presentation?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18 (18p-)
(B) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 21 (21q+)
(C) Trisomy of chromosome 13 (47,XX,+13)
(D) Translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21
(E) Monosomy of the X chromosome (45,X)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Advanced maternal age (38 years):** Increases the risk of chromosomal aneuploidies, including trisomies.
*   **Amniocentesis karyotype: 47,XX,+13:** This is the definitive genetic diagnosis, indicating an extra copy of chromosome 13 in a female fetus.
*   **Ultrasound findings:** Microcephaly, holoprosencephaly (alobar type), single umbilical artery, polydactyly, VSD. These are classic features associated with Trisomy 13.
*   **Physical exam findings at birth:** Prominent forehead, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, postaxial polydactyly. These further support the diagnosis of Trisomy 13.
*   **Neurological findings:** Severe hypotonia, absent deep tendon reflexes, severe central apnea leading to death. These reflect the severe neurological impairment characteristic of Trisomy 13, particularly the holoprosencephaly.
*   **Question asks for the genetic abnormality:** The vignette provides the karyotype result (47,XX,+13), which directly answers the question.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 13, also known as Patau syndrome, results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13. This leads to an overexpression of genes located on chromosome 13, disrupting normal development. The critical genes involved in the pathogenesis of Patau syndrome are thought to be located in the 13q14 region, which encodes several proteins involved in brain development, including SHH (Sonic Hedgehog) pathway components. Disruption of the SHH pathway is strongly implicated in the development of holoprosencephaly, a hallmark feature of Trisomy 13. Holoprosencephaly occurs due to failure of the prosencephalon (forebrain) to divide properly into two cerebral hemispheres, leading to a single ventricle and fusion of the cerebral lobes. Other features like cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, polydactyly, and cardiac defects are also associated with the overexpression of genes on chromosome 13, affecting various developmental processes. The karyotype 47,XX,+13 confirms the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 in a female individual, leading to the characteristic phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18 (18p-): This describes Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) or a partial monosomy 18. Edwards syndrome is characterized by severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. The specific features like holoprosencephaly and polydactyly are less common or absent in Trisomy 18 compared to Trisomy 13. The karyotype would be 45,XX,-18 or a deletion involving 18p. This option is incorrect because it describes a different chromosomal abnormality with a distinct clinical phenotype.
- (B) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 21 (21q+): This describes a partial trisomy 21. While full trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) is characterized by intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, and congenital heart defects, partial trisomy 21 (duplication of 21q) can present with a variable phenotype, sometimes overlapping with Down syndrome but often less severe or with different specific features. It does not typically cause the severe malformations like holoprosencephaly and polydactyly seen in Patau syndrome. The karyotype would show duplication of the long arm of chromosome 21. This option is incorrect because it describes a different chromosomal abnormality.
- (D) Translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21: This describes a Robertsonian translocation, often t(14;21), which can lead to Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) if the carrier parent passes on the translocated chromosome plus a normal chromosome 21. Down syndrome presents with different features than Patau syndrome (e.g., characteristic facial features, intellectual disability, congenital heart defects, but typically not holoprosencephaly or polydactyly). The karyotype would show the translocation, not a full trisomy 13. This option is incorrect because it describes a mechanism leading to a different trisomy.
- (E) Monosomy of the X chromosome (45,X): This describes Turner syndrome. Turner syndrome affects females and is characterized by short stature, ovarian dysgenesis (leading to infertility), webbed neck, lymphedema, and congenital heart defects (e.g., coarctation of the aorta). It does not cause the severe craniofacial and central nervous system malformations seen in Patau syndrome. The karyotype is 45,X. This option is incorrect because it describes a different chromosomal abnormality affecting a different sex with a completely different phenotype.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) is a severe autosomal trisomy characterized by specific congenital malformations, including holoprosencephaly, microcephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects. The diagnosis is confirmed by karyotyping showing 47,XX,+13 or 47,XY,+13. Differentiating it from other trisomies like Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) and Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) relies on recognizing the characteristic constellation of malformations. Key differentials include: Trisomy 18 (clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet), Trisomy 21 (characteristic facial features, intellectual disability), and Edwards syndrome (severe growth restriction, prominent occiput).

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13) - Craniofacial and CNS Malformations
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital malformations resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13, including holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia/cyclopia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects. The underlying mechanism involves gene dosage effects of critical genes located on chromosome 13, particularly those involved in early embryonic development and patterning. (Patau 综合征（13 号染色体三体）的特征是特定的先天性畸形，这是由于 13 号染色体多了一条拷贝造成的，包括全前脑发育不全、小眼球/独眼、唇裂/腭裂、多指症和心脏缺陷。潜在机制涉及位于 13 号染色体上关键基因的基因剂量效应，特别是那些参与早期胚胎发育和模式形成的基因。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Ultrasound reveals a fetus with a single ventricle heart, polydactyly on the left hand, severe cleft lip and palate, and microphthalmia. Amniocentesis is performed, and karyotyping of fetal cells shows 47,XY,+13. The parents are phenotypically normal and have no known family history of congenital anomalies. Given these findings, which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for the observed constellation of fetal abnormalities?

(A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21, leading to a Robertsonian translocation carrier state in the mother.
(B) A point mutation in the *SHH* gene on chromosome 7, causing impaired sonic hedgehog signaling during early development.
(C) A deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13, resulting in Patau syndrome with a specific subset of features.
(D) A maternal meiotic nondisjunction event during oogenesis, leading to an extra copy of chromosome 13 in the ovum.
(E) A uniparental disomy event for chromosome 13, where both copies are inherited from the father.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old primigravida (first pregnancy), suggesting potential for new chromosomal abnormalities.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Ultrasound at 16 weeks gestation identifies multiple severe fetal anomalies.
- **Specific Anomalies:**
    - Single ventricle heart: Common cardiac defect in Trisomy 13.
    - Polydactyly (left hand): Characteristic feature of Trisomy 13.
    - Severe cleft lip and palate: Common craniofacial defect in Trisomy 13.
    - Microphthalmia (small eyes): Characteristic feature of Trisomy 13, often associated with cyclopia (single eye) or anophthalmia (absence of eyes).
- **Diagnostic Test:** Amniocentesis confirms the karyotype: 47,XY,+13. This indicates Trisomy 13 in a male fetus.
- **Parental History:** Parents are phenotypically normal, suggesting a *de novo* event or a balanced rearrangement in one parent.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* molecular mechanism responsible for the observed abnormalities (Trisomy 13).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XY,+13 confirms the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 in a male fetus.
- **Mechanism of Trisomy:** The most common cause of trisomies (like Trisomy 13, 18, and 21) is nondisjunction during meiosis. Nondisjunction is the failure of homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) to separate properly.
- **Maternal vs. Paternal Nondisjunction:** Nondisjunction can occur during oogenesis (more common, increases with maternal age) or spermatogenesis. In this case, the fetus has 47,XY,+13. This means the ovum contributed two copies of chromosome 13 (due to nondisjunction) and the sperm contributed one normal copy (X or Y).
- **Option D:** Maternal meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis is the most frequent cause of Trisomy 13, accounting for ~90% of cases. The risk increases significantly with advanced maternal age. The fetus receives an egg cell with an extra chromosome 13 (n+1) and a normal sperm (n), resulting in a trisomic zygote (2n+1). This fits the clinical picture and the karyotype result.
- **Gene Dosage:** The extra copy of chromosome 13 leads to overexpression of genes located on that chromosome, disrupting normal developmental processes, particularly during early embryogenesis. Genes like *SHH* (Sonic Hedgehog), *TP53*, *WDR19*, *CDKN2C*, and *ATRX* are located on chromosome 13 and are implicated in the pathogenesis of Patau syndrome features like holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, and polydactyly.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21 would typically result in a different karyotype (e.g., 46,XX,t(13;21) or 47,XX,+13,t(13;21)). While translocations can cause trisomies, this specific scenario (13;21) is less common for Trisomy 13 itself and usually involves a carrier parent. The karyotype provided (47,XY,+13) is consistent with a free trisomy, not a translocation. Robertsonian translocations typically involve acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22), but a 13;21 translocation leading to a free +13 is less common than nondisjunction.
- (B): A point mutation in the *SHH* gene on chromosome 7 causes holoprosencephaly (HPE), a key feature of Patau syndrome. However, HPE can result from mutations in several genes (*SHH*, *PTCH1*, *GLI2*, *GLI3*, *ZIC2*, *SIX3*, *TDGF1*, *CDON*, *GAS1*, *PHOX2B*). While *SHH* mutations cause HPE, they do not explain the *entire* constellation of features seen in Trisomy 13 (polydactyly, cardiac defects, cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia) or the specific karyotype finding of +13. Furthermore, *SHH* is on chromosome 7, not 13.
- (C): A deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13 would result in a partial monosomy (e.g., 46,XY,del(13p)). This would lead to a *loss* of genetic material from 13p, causing a different set of clinical features (e.g., Patau syndrome-like features but typically less severe, or specific syndromes depending on the deleted region), not the *gain* of genetic material seen in Trisomy 13. The karyotype would show a deletion, not an extra chromosome.
- (E): Uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 13 means both copies of chromosome 13 are inherited from one parent (either mother or father). This can lead to imprinting disorders if the chromosome contains imprinted genes, but it does not typically cause the severe congenital malformations characteristic of Trisomy 13. The karyotype would be 46,XY,UPD(13) or 46,XX,UPD(13), not 47,XY,+13. UPD involves inheriting two copies from one parent and none from the other, resulting in a normal chromosome number but potential functional trisomy or monosomy for specific imprinted genes.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is most commonly caused by maternal meiotic nondisjunction, leading to an extra copy of chromosome 13. This results in gene dosage effects causing characteristic severe congenital anomalies.
- **Differentials:**
    - Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome): Caused by nondisjunction of chromosome 18; features include clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, cardiac defects.
    - Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome): Caused by nondisjunction of chromosome 21; features include intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, congenital heart defects, increased risk of leukemia and Alzheimer's disease.
    - Holoprosencephaly (HPE): Can be caused by mutations in various genes (including *SHH* on chromosome 7), but the specific combination of features (polydactyly, cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, cardiac defects) and the +13 karyotype point strongly to Trisomy 13.
    - Partial monosomies (e.g., del(13p)): Cause different syndromes due to loss of genetic material.
    - Translocations: Can cause trisomies but often involve a carrier parent and a different karyotype presentation.

<a id='question-078'></a>

### Question 078: Patau Syndrome Extremity and Cutaneous Signs: Postaxial Polydactyly (Extra Digits), Rocker-Bottom Feet, Aplasia Cutis Congenita (Scalp Punch-Out Lesion) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of characteristic clinical features associated with Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome), specifically the combination of postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita, linking these phenotypes to the underlying chromosomal abnormality. (本题考察的是13三体综合征（帕陶综合征）的特征性临床表现，特别是轴后多指症、摇椅底足和先天性皮肤缺损的组合，并将这些表型与潜在的染色体异常联系起来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn infant, born to a 32-year-old mother (gravida 2, para 1), presents with multiple congenital anomalies. Physical examination reveals bilateral postaxial polydactyly (extra digits on the ulnar side of each hand), bilateral rocker-bottom feet (convex plantar surface), and a large, well-demarcated, ulcerated lesion on the scalp consistent with aplasia cutis congenita. The infant also exhibits microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, and severe intellectual disability. Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes shows 47, XY, +13.

(A) The clinical features are most consistent with a deletion on the short arm of chromosome 18.
(B) The clinical features are most consistent with a translocation involving chromosomes 21 and 14.
(C) The clinical features are most consistent with a duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13.
(D) The clinical features are most consistent with a monosomy of the X chromosome in a female infant.
(E) The clinical features are most consistent with a point mutation in the *FGFR3* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn infant with multiple congenital anomalies:** Suggests a genetic syndrome.
- **Bilateral postaxial polydactyly:** Extra digits on the ulnar side (little finger side) of the hand. This is a characteristic feature of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).
- **Bilateral rocker-bottom feet:** Convex plantar surface, resembling a rocking chair bottom. Also a characteristic feature of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).
- **Aplasia cutis congenita:** Congenital absence of skin, often presenting as a punched-out lesion on the scalp. Strongly associated with Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).
- **Microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, severe intellectual disability:** Additional common features of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).
- **Karyotype analysis: 47, XY, +13:** Confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 13, establishing the diagnosis of Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome). The question asks for the genetic basis of the *clinical features* observed, which are consistent with the karyotype result.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome):** Caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 instead of the usual two. This can result from nondisjunction during meiosis (leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 13) or, less commonly, from a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 13.
- **Clinical Features:** The combination of postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita, cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, microcephaly, and severe intellectual disability is highly characteristic of Trisomy 13. These features arise from the overexpression of genes located on chromosome 13, disrupting normal developmental processes, particularly during embryogenesis. The specific genes involved in causing these particular phenotypes are complex and not fully elucidated, but the overall effect of having an extra copy of the entire chromosome 13 leads to this constellation of malformations.
- **Option C:** A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13 (13q duplication) would lead to an excess of genetic material from chromosome 13, similar to the effect of having a full extra chromosome 13. This would result in clinical features consistent with Patau syndrome, although the severity and specific manifestations might vary depending on the size and location of the duplication. The karyotype result (+13) directly implies an extra copy of the entire chromosome, which encompasses both the short (p) and long (q) arms. Therefore, a duplication of the long arm (13q) is a plausible underlying genetic mechanism leading to the observed phenotype, consistent with the overall effect of Trisomy 13.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deletion on the short arm of chromosome 18:** This describes a deletion in 18p, which is associated with **Edwards syndrome** (Trisomy 18) or **18p deletion syndrome** (also known as Nager syndrome or Townes-Brocks syndrome, depending on the specific location and size). Edwards syndrome features include microcephaly, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and severe intellectual disability, but typically *not* postaxial polydactyly or aplasia cutis congenita. 18p deletion syndrome features include microcephaly, micrognathia, low-set ears, and often limb abnormalities, but the specific combination described in the vignette is not typical. This option is incorrect because it describes a different chromosomal abnormality and associated syndrome.
- (B): **Translocation involving chromosomes 21 and 14:** This describes the most common chromosomal rearrangement associated with **Down syndrome** (Trisomy 21). Down syndrome features include characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), intellectual disability, congenital heart defects, and hypotonia. The clinical features described in the vignette (polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita) are not characteristic of Down syndrome. This option is incorrect because it describes the genetic basis for a different trisomy syndrome.
- (D): **Monosomy of the X chromosome in a female infant:** This describes **Turner syndrome** (45,X). Turner syndrome features include short stature, ovarian dysgenesis (leading to infertility), webbed neck, broad chest with widely spaced nipples, and lymphedema. The clinical features described in the vignette are completely inconsistent with Turner syndrome. This option is incorrect because it describes a different chromosomal abnormality and associated syndrome.
- (E): **Point mutation in the *FGFR3* gene:** Mutations in the *FGFR3* gene are associated with several skeletal dysplasias, most notably **achondroplasia** (most common form of dwarfism) and **Thanatophoric dysplasia** (a lethal skeletal dysplasia). Achondroplasia features include rhizomelic shortening of the limbs, macrocephaly, and characteristic facial features. Thanatophoric dysplasia is characterized by severe skeletal abnormalities, including short limbs, a narrow chest, and often stillbirth or early neonatal death. While limb abnormalities are present, the specific combination of postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita, and other features described in the vignette are not associated with *FGFR3* mutations. This option is incorrect because it describes a genetic cause for a different set of clinical features.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Recognize the classic triad of postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita as highly suggestive of Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome). Understand that trisomies are caused by an extra copy of a chromosome.
- **Differentials:** Differentiate Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) from Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) and Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) based on their distinct clinical features. Also, differentiate from single-gene disorders affecting limb development (like *FGFR3* mutations) and sex chromosome aneuploidies (like Turner syndrome).

<a id='question-079'></a>

### Question 079: Patau Syndrome Internal Organ Defects
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is associated with severe congenital anomalies, including holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, congenital heart defects (VSD, ASD, Tetralogy of Fallot), omphalocele, and renal abnormalities (polycystic kidneys), leading to early mortality. (Patau 综合征 (13 三体综合征) 与严重的先天性畸形相关，包括全前脑无裂症、小眼球症、唇裂/腭裂、多指症、先天性心脏缺陷 (VSD, ASD, Tetralogy of Fallot)、脐膨出和肾脏异常 (多囊肾)，导致早期死亡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis reveals an elevated maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein (MSAFP) level and shows fetal karyotype analysis of 47,XY,+13. Ultrasound confirms findings consistent with severe fetal anomalies, including holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, polydactyly on the left hand, and an omphalocele. Echocardiography reveals a large ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). Postnatally, the infant develops respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, and seizures. Renal ultrasound shows bilateral multicystic dysplastic kidneys. The infant dies within the first week of life.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is most likely responsible for the constellation of congenital anomalies observed in this infant?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18
(B) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 21
(C) Meiotic nondisjunction involving chromosome 13
(D) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21
(E) Uniparental disomy of chromosome 15

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida, 16 weeks gestation. This sets the context for prenatal diagnosis.
*   **Prenatal Screening:** Elevated MSAFP suggests potential neural tube defects or abdominal wall defects (like omphalocele).
*   **Karyotype:** 47,XY,+13 indicates Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) in a male fetus. This is the primary genetic diagnosis.
*   **Ultrasound Findings:** Holoprosencephaly (failure of the forebrain to divide), microphthalmia (small eyes), polydactyly (extra fingers/toes), omphalocele (abdominal organs herniate through the umbilicus). These are classic features of Trisomy 13.
*   **Echocardiography:** VSD and PDA are common congenital heart defects associated with Trisomy 13.
*   **Postnatal Course:** Respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, seizures, early death (within the first week) are typical outcomes for infants with Trisomy 13 due to the severity of the associated anomalies.
*   **Renal Ultrasound:** Bilateral multicystic dysplastic kidneys are another known feature of Trisomy 13.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the genetic abnormality responsible for the observed anomalies. The karyotype (47,XY,+13) directly points to the cause.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13 (either a full extra chromosome or a partial trisomy).
*   **Mechanism:** The most common cause (about 90%) is meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis (maternal origin), leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 13. Fertilization by a normal sperm results in a trisomic zygote. Paternal nondisjunction is less common. Mosaicism (some cells trisomic, others normal) and Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 13 are rarer causes.
*   **Phenotype:** The extra genetic material disrupts the normal development of multiple organ systems, leading to the characteristic severe congenital anomalies described in the vignette (brain, eyes, limbs, abdomen, heart, kidneys). The severity of the phenotype is often correlated with the type of trisomy (full trisomy > mosaicism > partial trisomy).
*   **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** Trisomy 13 is a classic example of an autosomal aneuploidy resulting from nondisjunction, leading to severe developmental defects and early mortality. It is frequently tested in the context of prenatal screening and diagnosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18:** This describes Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion). Features include a high-pitched cat-like cry, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and characteristic facial features. It does *not* cause the specific constellation of anomalies seen in the vignette (holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, polydactyly, VSD/PDA, multicystic kidneys).
*   **(B) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 21:** This describes a partial trisomy 21, often resulting from an unbalanced translocation or duplication. While it involves chromosome 21 (like Down syndrome), it typically presents with features of Down syndrome (intellectual disability, characteristic facies, heart defects like AVSD), but not the specific severe anomalies of Patau syndrome (holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, polydactyly, microphthalmia). A full trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) is caused by an extra copy of the entire chromosome 21, usually due to nondisjunction.
*   **(D) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21:** This is a common cause of familial Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). The carrier parent has a balanced translocation (e.g., t(14;21)), but can produce gametes with an extra chromosome 21 (leading to Down syndrome) or no chromosome 21 (leading to monosomy 21, which is usually lethal). This translocation involves chromosome 21, not 13, and causes Down syndrome features, not Patau syndrome.
*   **(E) Uniparental disomy of chromosome 15:** This refers to inheriting both copies of chromosome 15 from one parent and none from the other. Depending on the specific region and parental origin (maternal vs. paternal), it can cause Prader-Willi syndrome (paternal UPD of 15q11-q13) or Angelman syndrome (maternal UPD of 15q11-q13). These syndromes are characterized by intellectual disability, developmental delay, behavioral problems, and specific physical features, but not the severe congenital malformations of Patau syndrome.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 13, most commonly due to maternal meiotic nondisjunction. It presents with a severe phenotype including holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, congenital heart defects, omphalocele, and renal abnormalities, leading to early death. Differentiate it from Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome, associated with clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet, prominent occiput) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome, associated with intellectual disability, characteristic facies, AVSD).

<a id='question-080'></a>

### Question 080: Turner Syndrome (45,X0) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical manifestations, genetic basis (45,X0 karyotype), and associated biochemical/physiological consequences of Turner Syndrome, specifically related to ovarian dysgenesis and hormonal imbalances. (理解特纳综合征的临床表现、遗传基础（45,X0核型）以及相关的生化/生理后果，特别是卵巢发育不良和激素失衡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 14-year-old female presents to her pediatrician for evaluation of delayed puberty. She reports that her breasts have not developed, and she has not yet started menstruating, unlike her peers. Her past medical history is significant for recurrent otitis media in childhood and a history of coarctation of the aorta, which was surgically repaired at age 5. Physical examination reveals short stature (height at the 3rd percentile), a webbed neck, low posterior hairline, and widely spaced nipples. Her blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) levels (45 IU/L, normal range for pre-pubertal females <5 IU/L) and low serum estradiol levels (<20 pg/mL, normal range for pubertal females >100 pg/mL). Karyotype analysis reveals a 45,X0 chromosomal constitution in all peripheral blood lymphocytes.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the elevated FSH levels in this patient?

(A) Increased expression of the FSH receptor gene due to lack of estrogen signaling.
(B) Decreased expression of the FSH receptor gene due to lack of estrogen signaling.
(C) Increased negative feedback of estradiol on the hypothalamus and pituitary gland.
(D) Decreased negative feedback of estradiol on the hypothalamus and pituitary gland.
(E) Increased expression of the inhibin gene in the ovaries.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 14-year-old female with delayed puberty (no breast development, no menstruation). This points towards primary ovarian insufficiency or hypogonadism.
- **Past Medical History:** Recurrent otitis media, coarctation of the aorta. These are classic associated features of Turner Syndrome.
- **Physical Examination:** Short stature, webbed neck, low posterior hairline, widely spaced nipples. These are characteristic physical stigmata of Turner Syndrome.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated FSH, low estradiol. This pattern indicates primary ovarian failure (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism). The pituitary is trying to stimulate the ovaries (high FSH), but the ovaries are not responding adequately (low estradiol).
- **Karyotype:** 45,X0. This confirms the diagnosis of Turner Syndrome, caused by the complete or partial absence of one X chromosome.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the elevated FSH levels.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Turner Syndrome Pathophysiology:** The absence of a second X chromosome (or a significant portion of it) leads to haploinsufficiency of several genes crucial for ovarian development (e.g., *DAX1*, *NR0B1*, *SOX3*, *BMP15*, *GDF9*). This results in accelerated apoptosis of oogonia during fetal development, leading to streak ovaries (ovarian dysgenesis) and premature ovarian failure.
- **Hormonal Feedback Loop:** The hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis regulates reproductive function. The hypothalamus releases gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH), which stimulates the anterior pituitary to release luteinizing hormone (LH) and follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH). LH and FSH act on the ovaries to stimulate oocyte development and steroidogenesis, primarily the production of estradiol and progesterone. Estradiol exerts negative feedback on both the hypothalamus (reducing GnRH release) and the anterior pituitary (reducing LH and FSH release).
- **Mechanism in Turner Syndrome:** Due to ovarian dysgenesis, the ovaries in Turner Syndrome patients fail to produce sufficient estradiol. The lack of estradiol removes the negative feedback signal on the hypothalamus and pituitary gland. Consequently, the hypothalamus increases GnRH secretion, and the pituitary gland increases the secretion of LH and FSH in an attempt to stimulate the non-functional ovaries. This results in elevated serum levels of FSH and LH (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Increased expression of the FSH receptor gene due to lack of estrogen signaling. Estrogen deficiency does not typically lead to increased expression of the FSH receptor gene. While estrogen can influence receptor expression in some tissues, the primary mechanism for elevated FSH in primary ovarian failure is the loss of negative feedback, not upregulation of the receptor. Furthermore, even if receptor expression were increased, the ovaries are incapable of responding adequately due to the underlying developmental defect.
- (B) Decreased expression of the FSH receptor gene due to lack of estrogen signaling. Similar to (A), estrogen deficiency is not the primary driver of FSH receptor gene expression changes in this context. The core issue is the lack of ovarian response, not receptor downregulation.
- (C) Increased negative feedback of estradiol on the hypothalamus and pituitary gland. This is the opposite of what occurs in Turner Syndrome. The low estradiol levels *decrease* negative feedback, leading to increased FSH and LH secretion. Increased negative feedback would suppress FSH and LH.
- (E) Increased expression of the inhibin gene in the ovaries. Inhibin is a hormone produced by granulosa cells in the ovaries that selectively inhibits FSH secretion from the pituitary. In Turner Syndrome, the ovaries are dysgenetic and do not function properly, leading to *decreased* or absent inhibin production, not increased. The lack of inhibin contributes to the elevated FSH levels, but the primary driver is the loss of estradiol negative feedback.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Turner Syndrome (45,X0) is characterized by ovarian dysgenesis leading to primary ovarian failure (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism). The lack of estradiol production removes negative feedback on the HPG axis, resulting in elevated FSH and LH levels. Key differentials for hypergonadotropic hypogonadism include premature ovarian failure (POF) due to other causes (e.g., autoimmune oophoritis, chemotherapy, radiation) and androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS) with gonadal dysgenesis (though AIS typically presents with normal or high estrogen and high LH, FSH may be normal or slightly elevated). Understanding the HPG axis feedback loop is crucial.
