<a id='question-091'></a>

### Question 091: Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18 / 47,XX,+18)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of clinical features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome), a chromosomal abnormality resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18. The key is linking specific physical findings and developmental delays to this specific aneuploidy. (本题考察爱德华综合征（18三体综合征）的临床特征识别，这是一种染色体异常，由18号染色体多一条拷贝引起。关键是将特定的体格发现和发育迟缓与这种特定的染色体数目异常联系起来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotype analysis of the amniotic fluid cells reveals a female fetus with a 47,XX,+18 karyotype. At birth, the infant presents with micrognathia (small jaw), low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index finger over the middle finger, fifth finger over the fourth), rocker-bottom feet, and severe intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), weighing only 1.8 kg (below the 3rd percentile). Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). The infant develops severe feeding difficulties and respiratory distress shortly after birth. Chromosomal microarray analysis confirms the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18 material.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying genetic mechanism responsible for this infant's condition?

(A) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, leading to an extra copy of the critical region of chromosome 21.
(B) A meiotic nondisjunction event during oogenesis, resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 18.
(C) A de novo deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 5, leading to Cri-du-chat syndrome.
(D) A uniparental disomy event involving chromosome 15, resulting in Prader-Willi syndrome if inherited from the father or Angelman syndrome if inherited from the mother.
(E) A point mutation in the *CFTR* gene, leading to cystic fibrosis.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Advanced Maternal Age (38 years):** Increases the risk of meiotic nondisjunction events, which are the primary cause of aneuploidies like Trisomy 18.
*   **Amniocentesis Karyotype: 47,XX,+18:** This is the definitive diagnosis. It indicates a female fetus with three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two. This is Trisomy 18, also known as Edwards Syndrome.
*   **Clinical Features:** Micrognathia, low-set ears, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, IUGR, VSD, feeding difficulties, respiratory distress. These are classic physical and developmental findings associated with Trisomy 18.
*   **Chromosomal Microarray:** Confirms the presence of extra chromosome 18 material.

The question asks for the *underlying genetic mechanism* responsible for Trisomy 18. The karyotype directly shows the result (47,XX,+18), but the mechanism is how this karyotype arose.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is caused by the presence of a full or partial extra copy of chromosome 18. The most common cause (~95%) is **meiotic nondisjunction**, where homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) fail to separate properly during gamete formation (oogenesis or spermatogenesis). In this case, the mother is 38 years old, which is a significant risk factor for nondisjunction during oogenesis. Nondisjunction leads to a gamete (egg or sperm) with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1). If this gamete is fertilized by a normal gamete (n), the resulting zygote will have three copies of chromosome 18 (2n+1), leading to Trisomy 18. Less commonly (~1-2%), Trisomy 18 can result from **mitotic nondisjunction** occurring after fertilization, leading to mosaicism (some cells with 46 chromosomes, some with 47). Partial trisomy 18 can occur due to an isochromosome (loss of one arm of chromosome 18) or an unbalanced translocation. However, the standard karyotype 47,XX,+18 strongly suggests a full trisomy, most likely due to meiotic nondisjunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 typically leads to Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21), not Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18). The critical region for Down Syndrome is on chromosome 21. This option describes the mechanism for Down Syndrome.
- (C): A deletion of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 5 causes Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p- syndrome), characterized by a high-pitched, cat-like cry in infancy, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and distinctive facial features. This is a deletion syndrome, not a trisomy.
- (D): Uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 involves inheriting both copies of chromosome 15 from one parent and none from the other. If inherited from the father, it causes Prader-Willi syndrome (due to lack of expression of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region). If inherited from the mother, it causes Angelman syndrome (due to lack of expression of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region). This involves chromosome 15, not 18, and is a different type of genetic abnormality (imprinting disorder).
- (E): A point mutation in the *CFTR* gene causes cystic fibrosis, an autosomal recessive disorder affecting chloride transport, leading to thick mucus production in the lungs, pancreas, and other organs. This is a single-gene disorder, not a chromosomal aneuploidy, and affects a different gene and pathway.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction leading to an extra chromosome 18. Key clinical features include micrognathia, clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet, IUGR, and congenital heart defects. Differentiate it from Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome, caused by extra chromosome 21, often due to nondisjunction or translocation) and Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome, caused by extra chromosome 13). Also, differentiate it from deletion syndromes like Cri-du-chat (5p-) and single-gene disorders like cystic fibrosis (*CFTR* mutation) or imprinting disorders like Prader-Willi/Angelman (chromosome 15 UPD).

<a id='question-092'></a>

### Question 092: Edwards Syndrome Craniofacial Manifestations
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of characteristic craniofacial features associated with Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome), a chromosomal aneuploidy resulting from nondisjunction during meiosis. (本题考察爱德华综合征（18三体综合征）的特征性颅面部表现，这是一种由减数分裂过程中染色体分离不良导致的不整倍体。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) due to respiratory distress and feeding difficulties. She was born at 34 weeks gestation via cesarean section due to fetal distress. Her Apgar scores were 4 and 6 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. Physical examination reveals a low birth weight (1800g), microcephaly (head circumference 28 cm, <3rd percentile), a prominent occiput (back of the head), low-set and malformed ears, micrognathia (small jaw), clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index finger over middle finger, little finger over ring finger), and rocker-bottom feet. Echocardiography shows a ventricular septal defect (VSD). Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47,XX,+18.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying genetic mechanism responsible for the observed clinical features in this infant?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18.
(B) Translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21.
(C) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I resulting in an extra copy of chromosome 18 in the oocyte.
(D) Uniparental disomy of chromosome 18 inherited from the father.
(E) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Infant presentation:** 3-day-old female, premature (34 weeks), respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, low Apgar scores, low birth weight. These suggest a severe congenital condition.
- **Physical exam findings:** Microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set/malformed ears, micrognathia, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet. These are classic craniofacial and skeletal features strongly suggestive of Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18).
- **Cardiac finding:** Ventricular septal defect (VSD) is a common congenital heart defect associated with Trisomy 18.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18 confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 18 in a female infant. This indicates an extra copy of chromosome 18.
- **Question stem:** Asks for the underlying genetic mechanism causing the observed features (Trisomy 18).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two.
- The most common cause (approximately 95% of cases) is nondisjunction during meiosis, either meiosis I or meiosis II.
- Nondisjunction is the failure of homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) to separate properly during cell division.
- If nondisjunction occurs during oogenesis (maternal meiosis I or II), the resulting oocyte will have either 24 chromosomes with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1) or 23 chromosomes with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1).
- If nondisjunction occurs during spermatogenesis (paternal meiosis I or II), the resulting sperm will have either 24 chromosomes with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1) or 23 chromosomes with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1).
- Fertilization of a normal egg (23 chromosomes) by a sperm with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1) or a normal sperm (23 chromosomes) by an egg with an extra chromosome 18 (n+1) results in a zygote with 47 chromosomes, including three copies of chromosome 18 (47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18).
- The risk of nondisjunction increases with maternal age.
- The clinical features described (microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, micrognathia, clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet, VSD) are characteristic of Trisomy 18.
- The karyotype 47,XX,+18 confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 18.
- Option (C) describes nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I, which is a common mechanism leading to Trisomy 18. This results in an egg cell with an extra chromosome 18, which upon fertilization leads to a trisomic zygote.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18 (18p deletion) causes a different syndrome, often referred to as 18p syndrome or partial monosomy 18. While it involves chromosome 18, it results in a *loss* of genetic material, not an *extra* copy, and presents with different features (e.g., severe intellectual disability, distinctive facial features, but not typically the classic Edwards syndrome phenotype described). This is incorrect because the karyotype shows trisomy (+18), not deletion.
- (B): Translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21 would lead to Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13) or Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21) features, depending on the specific translocation and the resulting chromosome constitution. The clinical features described (microcephaly, prominent occiput, clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet) are characteristic of Trisomy 18, not 13 or 21. This is incorrect because the patient has Trisomy 18, not a translocation involving 13 and 21.
- (D): Uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 18 inherited from the father means the infant received both copies of chromosome 18 from the father and none from the mother. This is a form of diploidy (46 chromosomes) but with both copies originating from one parent. While UPD can sometimes cause developmental issues, it does not result in Trisomy 18 (47 chromosomes). Furthermore, paternal UPD of chromosome 18 is often associated with Silver-Russell syndrome, not Edwards syndrome. This is incorrect because the karyotype shows 47,XX,+18 (trisomy), not 46 chromosomes with UPD.
- (E): Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 is a common cause of familial Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21). It involves the fusion of the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21. While it can lead to Trisomy 21, it does not cause Trisomy 18. The clinical features described are those of Edwards Syndrome, not Down Syndrome. This is incorrect because the patient has Trisomy 18, not a Robertsonian translocation involving 14 and 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome) is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction, leading to an extra copy of chromosome 18. (18三体综合征（爱德华综合征）最常见的原因是减数分裂的不分离，导致第18号染色体多一份。)
- Key features include microcephaly, prominent occiput, low-set ears, micrognathia, clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. (主要特征包括小头畸形、枕骨隆起、低位耳朵、小下颌、握拳、摇椅底足和先天性心脏缺陷。)
- Differentiate Trisomy 18 from Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome - holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly) and Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome - flat facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, single palmar crease, intellectual disability). (区分18三体综合征与13三体综合征（全前脑无裂症、唇裂/腭裂、多指症）和21三体综合征（扁平面容、上斜眼裂、单指掌纹、智力障碍）。)

<a id='question-093'></a>

### Question 093: Edwards Syndrome Extremity Hallmark: Clenched Fists with Overlapping Fingers (Index Finger Overlapping 3rd, 5th Overlapping 4th) - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific physical findings, including clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, low-set ears, and congenital heart defects. This question tests the recognition of these characteristic features and their association with the underlying chromosomal abnormality.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotype analysis of the amniotic fluid cells reveals 47,XX,+18. Detailed fetal ultrasound shows intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), micrognathia, low-set ears, prominent occiput, and clenched fists with the index finger overlapping the third finger and the fifth finger overlapping the fourth finger. Echocardiography reveals a ventricular septal defect (VSD). The pregnancy is terminated. Post-mortem examination of the fetus confirms the ultrasound findings and reveals rocker-bottom feet. Which of the following genetic mechanisms is the most likely cause of this condition?

(A) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 14.
(B) A reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 21 and 22.
(C) Meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 18.
(D) A deletion of the short arm of chromosome 5.
(E) A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 32-year-old primigravida, advanced maternal age (38 years). Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for chromosomal nondisjunction.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Amniocentesis performed.
- **Karyotype:** 47,XX,+18. This directly indicates Trisomy 18, also known as Edwards syndrome. The presence of an extra chromosome 18 in a female fetus (XX) is the definitive diagnosis.
- **Ultrasound Findings:** IUGR, micrognathia, low-set ears, prominent occiput, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th), VSD, rocker-bottom feet. These are classic physical features associated with Trisomy 18. The specific hand deformity (clenched fists with overlapping fingers) is a hallmark sign.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely *genetic mechanism* causing this condition (Trisomy 18).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of three copies of chromosome 18 instead of the usual two.
- **Genetic Mechanisms:**
    - **Nondisjunction:** The most common cause (~95%) of trisomies. Occurs during meiosis I or meiosis II, leading to a gamete (egg or sperm) with an extra chromosome (n+1). If this gamete is fertilized by a normal gamete (n), the resulting zygote will have 2n+1 chromosomes (trisomy). Nondisjunction can occur during oogenesis (more common, especially with increasing maternal age) or spermatogenesis.
    - **Mosaicism:** Less common. Occurs post-zygotically due to nondisjunction during mitosis in early embryonic development. Results in some cells having the normal karyotype (46,XX or 46,XY) and others having the trisomic karyotype (47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18).
    - **Partial Trisomy:** Due to an isochromosome (two identical arms of a chromosome) or an unbalanced translocation. Less common than nondisjunction.
- **Maternal Age:** Risk of nondisjunction increases significantly with advanced maternal age. This patient is 38 years old, increasing the likelihood of meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis.
- **Conclusion:** Given the classic karyotype (47,XX,+18) and the patient's age, meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis leading to an egg with an extra chromosome 18 is the most probable mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 14 typically results in Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) or Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) if the carrier parent is unbalanced, or can lead to recurrent miscarriages or offspring with trisomy 13 or 21 if the carrier parent is balanced and the other parent contributes a normal chromosome 13 or 21. This mechanism does not cause Trisomy 18.
- (B): A reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 21 and 22 is a common cause of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) if the carrier parent contributes the translocated chromosome 21 along with a normal chromosome 21, or if the carrier parent is balanced and the other parent contributes a normal chromosome 21. This mechanism does not cause Trisomy 18.
- (D): A deletion of the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p-) causes Cri-du-chat syndrome, characterized by a high-pitched, cat-like cry in infancy, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and distinctive facial features. This is a deletion syndrome, not a trisomy.
- (E): A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13 would result in a partial trisomy 13q, which is rare and presents with features overlapping with Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), but not the specific constellation of findings seen in Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction, particularly during oogenesis in older mothers. Key features include clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, micrognathia, IUGR, and congenital heart defects. Differentiate from Down syndrome (Trisomy 21, caused by nondisjunction, translocation, or mosaicism; features include upslanting palpebral fissures, single palmar crease, intellectual disability) and Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13, caused by nondisjunction, translocation, or mosaicism; features include holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly). Cri-du-chat syndrome is a deletion (5p-), not a trisomy.

<a id='question-094'></a>

### Question 094: Edwards Syndrome Skeletal and Foot Anomalies: Rocker-Bottom Feet (Prominent Calcaneus), Short Sternum, Hypoplastic Nails - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies, including skeletal and foot deformities like rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, and hypoplastic nails, resulting from abnormal gene dosage of genes located on chromosome 18. (爱德华综合征（18三体综合征）的特征是特定的先天性异常，包括骨骼和足部畸形，如摇椅底足、短胸骨和指甲发育不良，这是由于18号染色体上基因剂量异常造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) with respiratory distress and feeding difficulties. She was born at 34 weeks gestation via cesarean section due to fetal distress. Her birth weight is 1.8 kg (below the 10th percentile). Physical examination reveals microcephaly, low-set ears, prominent occiput, clenched fists with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth), and bilateral rocker-bottom feet (prominent calcaneus). Chest X-ray shows a short sternum and bell-shaped thorax. Nail beds appear hypoplastic. Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47,XX,+18.

(A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21.
(B) A deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5.
(C) A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13.
(D) A trisomy of chromosome 18.
(E) A monosomy of the X chromosome with a structural abnormality of chromosome 13.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** A neonate presenting with respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, and multiple congenital anomalies. This points towards a chromosomal abnormality or severe genetic syndrome.
- **Physical Findings:** Microcephaly, low-set ears, prominent occiput, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet (prominent calcaneus), short sternum, bell-shaped thorax, and hypoplastic nails are classic features of Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18).
- **Karyotype:** The karyotype result of 47,XX,+18 directly confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 18 in a female infant.
- **Question Stem:** The question asks for the underlying genetic abnormality based on the clinical presentation and karyotype results.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Edwards syndrome, also known as Trisomy 18, is a genetic disorder caused by the presence of a third copy of chromosome 18, either in whole or in part.
- The standard karyotype is 47,XX,+18 (female) or 47,XY,+18 (male).
- The clinical features described in the vignette (microcephaly, low-set ears, prominent occiput, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, bell-shaped thorax, hypoplastic nails, respiratory distress, feeding difficulties, low birth weight) are highly characteristic of Trisomy 18.
- The presence of an extra chromosome 18 leads to an overexpression of genes located on that chromosome, disrupting normal development, particularly affecting skeletal, cardiac, central nervous system, and other organ systems.
- The specific skeletal anomalies like rocker-bottom feet (due to prominent calcaneus and talus), short sternum, and hypoplastic nails are direct consequences of abnormal gene dosage affecting skeletal development genes on chromosome 18.
- The karyotype result (47,XX,+18) definitively confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 18.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21 would result in Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) or Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), depending on the specific translocation (e.g., Robertsonian translocation t(13;21)). The clinical features described (especially rocker-bottom feet, prominent occiput, clenched fists) are more typical of Trisomy 18, not 13 or 21. Down syndrome features include flat facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, single palmar crease, and intellectual disability. Patau syndrome features include holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and severe intellectual disability.
- (B): A deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p-) causes Cri-du-chat syndrome. This syndrome is characterized by a high-pitched, cat-like cry in infancy, microcephaly, intellectual disability, and distinctive facial features (e.g., hypertelorism, epicanthal folds). The skeletal and foot anomalies described in the vignette are not characteristic of Cri-du-chat syndrome.
- (C): A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13 (13q duplication) is a rare chromosomal abnormality. While it can cause developmental delay and congenital anomalies, the specific constellation of features seen in the vignette (rocker-bottom feet, short sternum, clenched fists, prominent occiput) is highly specific for Trisomy 18, not a 13q duplication.
- (E): A monosomy of the X chromosome (Turner syndrome, 45,X) affects females and is characterized by short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, webbed neck, and cardiac defects (e.g., coarctation of the aorta). A structural abnormality of chromosome 13 would point towards Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), but the combination of X monosomy and a chromosome 13 abnormality does not fit the clinical picture or the provided karyotype (47,XX,+18).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) is characterized by a specific set of congenital anomalies, including rocker-bottom feet, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, short sternum, microcephaly, prominent occiput, and severe developmental delay. The diagnosis is confirmed by karyotype analysis showing 47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18. Key differentials include Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) and Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), which have distinct clinical features. Cri-du-chat syndrome (5p deletion) and Turner syndrome (45,X) present with different clinical pictures.

### Question 095: Edwards Syndrome Internal Organ Defects: Ventricular Septal Defect (VSD), Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA), Horseshoe Kidney, Meckel Diverticulum - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is associated with specific congenital anomalies, including cardiac defects (VSD, PDA), renal abnormalities (horseshoe kidney), and gastrointestinal issues (Meckel diverticulum), resulting from abnormal development during embryogenesis due to the extra chromosome 18. (爱德华综合征（18三体综合征）与特定的先天性异常相关，包括心脏缺陷（VSD、PDA）、肾脏异常（马蹄肾）和胃肠道问题（甲状突起），这是由于胚胎发育期间额外18号染色体导致的异常发展。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant, born to a 32-year-old mother, presents with failure to thrive, developmental delay, and recurrent respiratory infections. Prenatal ultrasound showed intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR). At birth, the infant exhibited microcephaly, low-set ears, clenched hands with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth), and a prominent occiput. Echocardiography revealed a ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). Renal ultrasound showed a horseshoe kidney. Gastrointestinal evaluation revealed a Meckel diverticulum. Karyotyping performed on the infant confirmed the diagnosis. Which of the following genetic abnormalities is most likely responsible for this constellation of findings?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 13 (13p-)
(B) Translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21
(C) Trisomy 18
(D) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 22 (22q11.2 duplication)
(E) Monosomy X (Turner syndrome)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Infant presentation:** Failure to thrive, developmental delay, recurrent respiratory infections, microcephaly, low-set ears, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, prominent occiput. These are classic dysmorphic features and clinical signs associated with a specific chromosomal abnormality.
- **Prenatal findings:** IUGR is common in chromosomal abnormalities affecting growth.
- **Specific congenital anomalies:** VSD, PDA (cardiac defects), horseshoe kidney (renal anomaly), Meckel diverticulum (gastrointestinal anomaly). This specific combination of defects is highly characteristic.
- **Karyotyping confirmation:** The diagnosis was confirmed by karyotyping, indicating a chromosomal abnormality.
- **Question:** Asks for the specific genetic abnormality responsible for this clinical picture.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (dysmorphic features, developmental delay, IUGR) combined with the specific constellation of congenital anomalies (VSD, PDA, horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum) strongly points towards Edwards syndrome, also known as Trisomy 18.
- Trisomy 18 results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 18. This extra genetic material disrupts the normal developmental processes, leading to the characteristic phenotype.
- The genes located on chromosome 18 are crucial for various developmental pathways, including cardiac development, kidney formation, and gastrointestinal development. Dosage sensitivity of these genes leads to the observed malformations.
- The specific combination of VSD, PDA, horseshoe kidney, and Meckel diverticulum is a well-documented association with Trisomy 18.
- Other features like clenched hands with overlapping fingers (index over middle, fifth over fourth) and rocker-bottom feet are also characteristic of Trisomy 18.
- The underlying mechanism involves the overexpression of genes on chromosome 18, disrupting normal gene regulation and cellular processes during embryogenesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 13 (13p-) causes Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13). Patau syndrome presents with severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects (like VSD, ASD, PDA), but the specific combination of horseshoe kidney and Meckel diverticulum is not characteristic, and the dysmorphic features (e.g., clenched hands) are different.
- (B) Translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 typically leads to Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), although it can also cause other conditions depending on the specific breakpoint and genes involved. Down syndrome features include intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), single palmar crease, congenital heart defects (AVSD, VSD), and increased risk of leukemia and Alzheimer's disease. Horseshoe kidney and Meckel diverticulum are not typical features, and the dysmorphic features are distinct from those described.
- (D) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 22 (22q11.2 duplication) is much rarer than the deletion syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome, DiGeorge syndrome). 22q11.2 deletion syndrome involves features like cardiac defects (conotruncal defects like tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), hypocalcemia (due to parathyroid hypoplasia), immune deficiency (thymic hypoplasia), and characteristic facial features. While cardiac defects are present, the specific combination of anomalies (horseshoe kidney, Meckel diverticulum) and the overall phenotype (e.g., clenched hands) do not match. 22q11.2 duplication syndrome is associated with developmental delay, autism spectrum disorder, and schizophrenia, but the specific congenital anomalies differ significantly.
- (E) Monosomy X (Turner syndrome) affects females (45,X). Features include short stature, ovarian dysgenesis (leading to infertility and lack of secondary sexual characteristics), webbed neck, low posterior hairline, lymphedema, and cardiac defects (coarctation of the aorta, bicuspid aortic valve). Horseshoe kidney can occur, but VSD, PDA, and Meckel diverticulum are not typical features, and the dysmorphic features are different. The karyotype is also distinct (45,X vs. Trisomy 18).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is characterized by a specific constellation of congenital anomalies including cardiac defects (VSD, PDA), renal anomalies (horseshoe kidney), and gastrointestinal anomalies (Meckel diverticulum), along with characteristic dysmorphic features and severe developmental delay. (爱德华综合征（18三体综合征）的特点是特定先天性异常的组合，包括心脏缺陷（VSD、PDA）、肾脏异常（马蹄肾）和胃肠道异常（甲状突起），以及特征性面部畸形和严重的智力发育迟缓。)
- Differentiate Trisomy 18 from other trisomies (Trisomy 13 - Patau syndrome, Trisomy 21 - Down syndrome) and other chromosomal abnormalities (Turner syndrome, DiGeorge syndrome) based on the specific clinical features and associated anomalies. (根据具体临床特征和相关异常将18三体综合征与其他三体综合征（13三体综合征、21三体综合征）以及其他染色体异常（特纳综合征、迪乔治综合征）区分开。)

<a id='question-096'></a>

### Question 096: Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13 / 47,XX,+13)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical features, cytogenetic basis, and associated developmental defects characteristic of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), specifically focusing on the impact of an extra chromosome 13 on midline forebrain and craniofacial development.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed due to advanced maternal age (38 years). Karyotyping of the amniotic fluid cells reveals a 47,XX,+13 karyotype. Ultrasound findings show severe fetal anomalies, including holoprosencephaly (alobar type), microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, polydactyly (postaxial on both hands), and rocker-bottom feet. The parents are counseled regarding the prognosis, which is typically poor, with most affected infants dying within the first year of life due to severe congenital malformations. Given the cytogenetic findings and associated clinical features, which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the developmental abnormalities observed in this fetus?

(A) A translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 21, leading to an unbalanced karyotype with trisomy 13 and monosomy 21.
(B) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 14, resulting in an unbalanced karyotype with trisomy 13.
(C) A point mutation in the *SHH* gene on chromosome 7, disrupting sonic hedgehog signaling crucial for midline development.
(D) A duplication of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13, leading to an increased dosage of genes located in this region, particularly those involved in forebrain patterning.
(E) A meiotic nondisjunction event during oogenesis, resulting in a gamete with an extra copy of chromosome 13, which was then fertilized by a normal sperm.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old primigravida, 16 weeks gestation, advanced maternal age (38 years). Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidies.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Amniocentesis performed.
- **Cytogenetic Result:** 47,XX,+13. This indicates a female fetus (XX) with an extra copy of chromosome 13 (trisomy 13).
- **Ultrasound Findings:** Holoprosencephaly (alobar type), microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet. These are classic features associated with Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13). Holoprosencephaly, in particular, is strongly linked to defects in midline forebrain development.
- **Prognosis:** Poor, with high infant mortality. This is consistent with the severity of Patau syndrome.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely molecular mechanism responsible for the developmental abnormalities.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome):** Caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13. The most common cause is meiotic nondisjunction, where homologous chromosomes or sister chromatids fail to separate properly during meiosis I or meiosis II, respectively.
- **Nondisjunction:** Can occur during oogenesis (more common, especially with advanced maternal age) or spermatogenesis. If nondisjunction occurs during oogenesis, an egg cell will have either 24 chromosomes (nullisomy 13) or 25 chromosomes (+13). If fertilized by a normal sperm (23 chromosomes), the resulting zygote will have 47 chromosomes (monosomy 13 or trisomy 13).
- **Clinical Features:** The specific features observed (holoprosencephaly, cleft lip/palate, microphthalmia, polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet) are characteristic consequences of the extra genetic material on chromosome 13 disrupting normal embryonic development, particularly affecting midline structures and limb development. Genes on chromosome 13, such as *SHH* (Sonic Hedgehog) and *TP53*, play critical roles in these processes. However, the *primary* mechanism causing the trisomy itself is nondisjunction.
- **Option E:** Directly describes the most common mechanism (meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis) leading to the observed 47,XX,+13 karyotype. Advanced maternal age increases the risk of oocyte nondisjunction. This mechanism explains the presence of the extra chromosome 13, which is the root cause of the syndrome's features.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 21 would typically result in a karyotype like 46,XX,t(13;21)(q14;q10) or 47,XX,+13,+21 (if unbalanced). While translocations can cause trisomies, this specific scenario (trisomy 13 *and* monosomy 21) is highly unlikely and doesn't fit the given karyotype (47,XX,+13). Furthermore, the classic features described are primarily associated with trisomy 13, not a combined trisomy 13/monosomy 21.
- (B): A Robertsonian translocation involves the long arms of two acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22). A translocation between chromosome 13 and 14 (t(13;14)) could lead to an unbalanced karyotype with trisomy 13 (e.g., 46,XX,t(13;14)(q14;q14);47,XX,+13), but the karyotype provided is simply 47,XX,+13, which is the result of nondisjunction, not a translocation. Robertsonian translocations typically involve chromosomes 13, 14, 15, 21, and 22.
- (C): A point mutation in the *SHH* gene (located on chromosome 7) causes holoprosencephaly, but it does not explain the *trisomy 13* karyotype (47,XX,+13). While genes on chromosome 13 (like *SHH* itself, although *SHH* is on chromosome 7) contribute to holoprosencephaly, the underlying cause here is the extra chromosome 13, not a mutation in a gene on a different chromosome. This option confuses the *consequence* (holoprosencephaly) with the *cause* (trisomy 13).
- (D): A duplication of the short arm (p arm) of chromosome 13 would result in a partial trisomy 13p, which has a different phenotype (e.g., Patau syndrome with cleft lip/palate, but typically *without* holoprosencephaly or severe polydactyly). The karyotype 47,XX,+13 indicates a full trisomy 13, involving the entire chromosome, not just the p arm.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction, particularly during oogenesis in older mothers, leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 13. The resulting karyotype is 47,XX,+13 or 47,XY,+13.
- **Differentials:** Other causes of trisomy 13 include mosaicism (less severe phenotype) and, rarely, Robertsonian translocations or isochromosome 13q. However, nondisjunction is the most frequent mechanism. Distinguish Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) from Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) and Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) based on their characteristic clinical features and associated genes. Also, differentiate trisomy from partial trisomies (e.g., 13p duplication) or translocations.

<a id='question-097'></a>

### Question 097: Patau Syndrome Hallmarks: Holoprosencephaly (Failure of Forebrain Bifurcation), Cyclopia/Microphthalmia, Severe Cleft Lip and Palate - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies resulting from the overexpression of genes located on chromosome 13, particularly those involved in early embryonic development, including forebrain patterning and facial development. (Patau 综合征（13 三体综合征）的特点是特定的先天性异常，这是由于 13 号染色体上基因的过度表达造成的，特别是那些参与早期胚胎发育的基因，包括前脑模式形成和面部发育。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis reveals an abnormal karyotype: 47,XX,+13. Ultrasound shows severe fetal anomalies, including holoprosencephaly (failure of the prosencephalon to divide into two cerebral hemispheres), microphthalmia (abnormally small eyes), a prominent occiput, a short nose, and a severe cleft lip and palate. The fetus also exhibits polydactyly (extra fingers/toes) on both hands and feet, and cardiac defects, specifically a ventricular septal defect (VSD) and atrial septal defect (ASD). Given the constellation of findings associated with this chromosomal abnormality, which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed developmental defects?

(A) Haploinsufficiency of the *SHH* gene on chromosome 7, leading to impaired ventral midline development.
(B) Dosage-sensitive overexpression of genes located on the extra chromosome 13, including *TP53*, disrupting normal embryonic patterning and cell differentiation.
(C) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 13, resulting in the loss of imprinting and activation of paternally expressed genes involved in growth restriction.
(D) A translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 21, leading to a mosaic karyotype with varying levels of gene expression from both chromosomes.
(E) A de novo mutation in the *FGFR3* gene on chromosome 4, causing constitutive activation of the receptor tyrosine kinase and abnormal bone growth.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old primigravida at 16 weeks gestation.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Amniocentesis reveals karyotype 47,XX,+13 (Trisomy 13).
- **Ultrasound Findings:**
    - Holoprosencephaly: Failure of forebrain division.
    - Microphthalmia: Small eyes.
    - Prominent occiput, short nose, severe cleft lip/palate: Characteristic facial dysmorphisms.
    - Polydactyly: Extra digits.
    - Cardiac defects (VSD, ASD): Common congenital heart defects.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely molecular mechanism responsible for these developmental defects.
- **Key Clue:** The karyotype (47,XX,+13) directly indicates Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome). The specific constellation of severe congenital anomalies (holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, cardiac defects) are classic features of this syndrome. The question asks for the *mechanism* by which this extra chromosome causes these defects.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Trisomy 13 involves the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 instead of the usual two.
- This leads to an increased dosage (overexpression) of all genes located on chromosome 13.
- Many genes on chromosome 13 are critical for normal embryonic development, particularly during early stages.
- Overexpression of these genes disrupts the delicate balance of signaling pathways and transcription factors required for proper cell differentiation, migration, and morphogenesis.
- Specific genes implicated include *TP53* (tumor protein p53), *PATX* (pituitary aborted transcription factor 1), *SHH* (sonic hedgehog), *GLI3*, *SIX3*, and others.
- *TP53* is a tumor suppressor gene, but its overexpression during development can interfere with cell cycle control and apoptosis, contributing to developmental abnormalities.
- *PATX* is specifically involved in forebrain development, and its overexpression is strongly linked to holoprosencephaly.
- *SHH* and *GLI3* are crucial for ventral midline patterning, including facial development and forebrain cleavage. Overexpression can disrupt this process.
- The severity of the phenotype in Trisomy 13 is often attributed to the dosage-sensitive nature of these developmental genes. A single extra copy leads to significant disruption.
- Therefore, the overexpression of multiple genes on chromosome 13, disrupting normal developmental processes, is the primary molecular mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Haploinsufficiency of the *SHH* gene (located on chromosome 7) causes holoprosencephaly, but this is typically due to a *loss* of function (e.g., mutation, deletion) of *one* copy of the gene, not overexpression of genes on chromosome 13. Patau syndrome involves *overexpression* of genes on chromosome 13, including *SHH*, but the primary mechanism is the overall gene dosage effect of the entire trisomy, not haploinsufficiency of a gene on a different chromosome.
- (C): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 13 would result in two copies of the chromosome inherited from the mother and none from the father. This can lead to specific syndromes depending on the imprinted genes on chromosome 13, but it doesn't typically cause the classic Patau syndrome phenotype with holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects. UPD is a mechanism of *disomy* (two copies), not *trisomy* (three copies).
- (D): A translocation involving chromosome 13 and chromosome 21 would lead to Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or a different chromosomal abnormality depending on the specific translocation breakpoints. While mosaicism can occur, the karyotype given is 47,XX,+13, indicating a full trisomy 13, not a translocation resulting in a mosaic.
- (E): A de novo mutation in the *FGFR3* gene (located on chromosome 4) causes achondroplasia (a form of dwarfism) due to constitutive activation leading to abnormal cartilage and bone development. This is unrelated to the features of Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) like holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13, leading to overexpression of genes critical for embryonic development. This dosage effect disrupts normal patterning and morphogenesis, causing characteristic severe congenital anomalies like holoprosencephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, and cardiac defects. Key differentials include Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), which have distinct clinical features and involve different chromosomes. Holoprosencephaly can also be caused by mutations in genes like *SHH*, *PTCH1*, *GLI3*, or *SIX3*, but in the context of a confirmed Trisomy 13 karyotype, the mechanism is gene dosage effect.

<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Patau Syndrome Extremity and Cutaneous Signs
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is characterized by specific congenital anomalies including postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita, resulting from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13. (Patau 综合征 (13 号染色体三体) 的特征是特定的先天性异常，包括轴后多指症、摇椅底足和先天性皮肤缺损，这是由于 13 号染色体多了一条拷贝造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn infant, born to a 32-year-old mother (gravida 2, para 1), presents with multiple congenital anomalies shortly after birth. Physical examination reveals a prominent forehead, microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip and palate, and cardiac defects (ventricular septal defect and patent ductus arteriosus). Examination of the extremities shows postaxial polydactyly (extra digits on the ulnar side of the hands and feet) and bilateral rocker-bottom feet. A distinct, circular, full-thickness skin defect is noted on the scalp, described as an aplasia cutis congenita lesion ("punch-out" lesion). Karyotype analysis performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals 47, XX, +13.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is the underlying cause of this infant's clinical presentation?

(A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21.
(B) A deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18.
(C) A duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13.
(D) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21.
(E) A point mutation in the *FGFR3* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn infant with multiple congenital anomalies:** Suggests a genetic syndrome.
- **Prominent forehead, microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, cardiac defects (VSD, PDA):** Classic features associated with Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome).
- **Postaxial polydactyly:** Extra digits on the ulnar side, a highly characteristic feature of Patau syndrome.
- **Rocker-bottom feet:** Convex plantar surface, another characteristic feature of Patau syndrome.
- **Aplasia cutis congenita (scalp "punch-out" lesion):** A specific skin defect commonly seen in Patau syndrome.
- **Karyotype: 47, XX, +13:** Confirms the presence of an extra chromosome 13 in a female infant. This is the definitive diagnosis of Trisomy 13.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (holoprosencephaly features like microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, cardiac defects, polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, aplasia cutis congenita) is highly suggestive of Patau syndrome.
- The karyotype result (47, XX, +13) confirms Trisomy 13.
- Trisomy 13 results from the presence of three copies of chromosome 13 instead of the usual two.
- This extra genetic material disrupts the normal development of multiple organ systems, leading to the observed constellation of congenital anomalies.
- The most common cause of Trisomy 13 is nondisjunction during meiosis (either maternal or paternal), leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 13. Fertilization of a normal gamete by this abnormal gamete results in the trisomy.
- Less commonly, Trisomy 13 can result from an unbalanced translocation involving chromosome 13, or rarely, mosaicism.
- The question asks for the *underlying cause* of the clinical presentation, which is the presence of an extra chromosome 13.
- Option (C) describes a duplication of the long arm of chromosome 13. While a duplication of the *entire* chromosome 13 is the most common mechanism (due to nondisjunction), a duplication specifically of the long arm (q arm) could also lead to a phenotype consistent with Patau syndrome, although often less severe depending on the size and location of the duplication. However, given the classic full Patau syndrome phenotype and the karyotype result showing a full extra chromosome 13, a duplication of the *entire* chromosome 13 (or the long arm, which contains most of the critical genes) is the most fitting molecular explanation among the choices provided. Nondisjunction leading to a full trisomy is the most frequent cause, which functionally results in having three copies of the entire chromosome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) A translocation involving chromosomes 13 and 21: This describes a Robertsonian translocation, often t(13;21). While it *can* lead to an unbalanced karyotype with Trisomy 13 (if the carrier parent passes on the translocated chromosome 13 plus a normal chromosome 21, and a normal chromosome 13 from the other parent), it is not the *most common* cause of Patau syndrome, which is typically due to nondisjunction resulting in a full trisomy 13. Furthermore, this option doesn't directly describe the *cause* of the phenotype in *this* infant, whose karyotype shows 47, XX, +13, implying a full extra chromosome, not necessarily a translocation.
- (B) A deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18: This describes the genetic basis for Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), specifically the deletion syndrome 18p-. Edwards syndrome presents with a different set of features (e.g., clenched fists with overlapping fingers, prominent occiput, low-set ears, rocker-bottom feet, severe intellectual disability, cardiac defects), although rocker-bottom feet can be seen in both Patau and Edwards syndromes. The clinical features described in the vignette (polydactyly, aplasia cutis congenita, specific facial features) are much more characteristic of Patau syndrome.
- (D) A Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21: This describes a translocation, often t(14;21), which is the most common cause of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). Down syndrome has a distinct phenotype (e.g., upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, single palmar crease, intellectual disability, congenital heart defects). This is clearly not the diagnosis in this case.
- (E) A point mutation in the *FGFR3* gene: Mutations in the *FGFR3* gene are associated with skeletal dysplasias, particularly achondroplasia (most common form of dwarfism) and hypochondroplasia. These conditions primarily affect bone growth and development, leading to short stature and limb abnormalities, but not the constellation of severe central nervous system, facial, cardiac, and cutaneous defects seen in Patau syndrome. Polydactyly can sometimes be seen in achondroplasia, but it's typically preaxial, not postaxial, and the other features are completely different.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 13, most commonly due to meiotic nondisjunction. It presents with characteristic features including holoprosencephaly spectrum defects (microcephaly, microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate), cardiac defects, postaxial polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and aplasia cutis congenita.
- Key differentials include Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18) and Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), each with distinct clinical features and associated genetic mechanisms (nondisjunction, translocation, mosaicism). Skeletal dysplasias like achondroplasia (FGFR3 mutation) present with different limb and growth abnormalities.

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Patau Syndrome Internal Organ Defects
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome) is associated with severe congenital anomalies, including characteristic facial features (holoprosencephaly), cardiac defects (VSD, ASD, Tetralogy of Fallot), renal abnormalities (polycystic kidneys), abdominal wall defects (omphalocele), and early mortality. The underlying cause is an extra copy of chromosome 13. (Patau 综合征 (13 号染色体三体) 与严重的先天性畸形相关，包括特征性面部特征（前脑无裂症）、心脏缺陷（室间隔缺损、房间隔缺损、四联症）、肾脏异常（多囊肾）、腹壁缺损（脐膨出）和早期死亡。根本原因是 13 号染色体多一份拷贝。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis reveals an abnormal karyotype: 47,XX,+13. Ultrasound shows fetal polyhydramnios, microcephaly, holoprosencephaly, an omphalocele containing bowel loops, and a single umbilical artery. Echocardiography demonstrates a large ventricular septal defect (VSD) and a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA). Postnatally, the infant exhibits severe developmental delay, seizures, and microphthalmia. Renal ultrasound confirms bilateral multicystic dysplastic kidneys. The infant dies within the first week of life due to respiratory failure and cardiac complications.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is the underlying cause of this infant's constellation of findings?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18
(B) Meiotic nondisjunction resulting in trisomy 21
(C) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21
(D) Meiotic nondisjunction resulting in trisomy 13
(E) Uniparental disomy of chromosome 15

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Prenatal Screening & Amniocentesis:** Abnormal karyotype 47,XX,+13 indicates an extra chromosome 13 in a female fetus. This is the primary genetic finding.
- **Ultrasound Findings:** Polyhydramnios (excess amniotic fluid) can be associated with fetal swallowing difficulties or increased urine production (though renal function is often impaired). Microcephaly (small head), holoprosencephaly (failure of the forebrain to divide properly), omphalocele (abdominal organs herniate through the umbilicus), and a single umbilical artery are classic features associated with specific chromosomal abnormalities.
- **Echocardiography:** VSD and PDA are common congenital heart defects.
- **Postnatal Findings:** Severe developmental delay, seizures, microphthalmia (small eyes), and multicystic dysplastic kidneys are further characteristic features.
- **Early Death:** High mortality rate in infancy is typical for severe trisomies.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of the specific karyotype (47,XX,+13) and the characteristic constellation of congenital anomalies (holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, VSD, polycystic kidneys, microcephaly, microphthalmia, developmental delay, early death) strongly points to Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome):** This condition results from the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 13.
- **Mechanism:** The most common cause is meiotic nondisjunction during oogenesis (maternal origin, ~90%) or spermatogenesis (paternal origin, ~10%), leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 13. Fertilization of a normal gamete by this aneuploid gamete results in a trisomic zygote. Mosaicism (some cells trisomic, others normal) can also occur but usually results in a milder phenotype.
- **Phenotype:** The extra genetic material from chromosome 13 disrupts the normal development of multiple organ systems. Key features include:
    - **Central Nervous System:** Holoprosencephaly (severe brain malformation), microcephaly, intellectual disability.
    - **Face:** Microphthalmia, cleft lip/palate, sloping forehead.
    - **Heart:** Congenital heart defects (VSD, ASD, PDA, Tetralogy of Fallot).
    - **Abdomen:** Omphalocele, polydactyly (extra fingers/toes), single umbilical artery.
    - **Kidneys:** Polycystic kidneys, renal agenesis.
- **Molecular Basis:** The specific genes on chromosome 13 involved in causing these defects are numerous and complex, reflecting the widespread developmental roles of genes located on this chromosome. The dosage effect of these genes (having three copies instead of two) leads to abnormal protein expression and disrupted developmental pathways.
- **Inheritance:** Trisomy 13 is typically a sporadic event due to nondisjunction and is not inherited, although parental chromosomal rearrangements (like Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 13) can increase the risk.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18: This describes Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), specifically the deletion syndrome (18p-). Edwards syndrome is characterized by severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. While there are some overlapping features (microcephaly, heart defects), the specific combination of holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, and polycystic kidneys is much more characteristic of Trisomy 13. The karyotype is also incorrect (should be 47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18, or a deletion like 46,XX,del(18p)).
- (B) Meiotic nondisjunction resulting in trisomy 21: This describes Down syndrome. Down syndrome is characterized by intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), congenital heart defects (AVSD, VSD), increased risk of leukemia and Alzheimer's disease, and duodenal atresia. Holoprosencephaly and omphalocele are not typical features of Down syndrome. The karyotype is 47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21.
- (C) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21: This describes a common cause of familial Down syndrome. While it can lead to trisomy 21 (and thus Down syndrome phenotype), it does not cause the specific features described in the vignette (holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, polycystic kidneys). The karyotype would be complex, involving a balanced translocation carrier parent and an unbalanced translocation leading to trisomy 21 in the offspring (e.g., t(14;21)(q10;q10); 47,XY,+21).
- (E) Uniparental disomy of chromosome 15: This refers to inheriting both copies of chromosome 15 from one parent and none from the other. Depending on the parental origin (maternal vs. paternal) and whether it involves the imprinting region (15q11-q13), it can cause Prader-Willi syndrome (paternal UPD) or Angelman syndrome (maternal UPD). These syndromes are characterized by distinct neurodevelopmental and behavioral features, obesity (Prader-Willi), seizures (Angelman), and specific facial features, none of which match the vignette's presentation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Recognize the classic clinical features (holoprosencephaly, omphalocele, VSD, polycystic kidneys) and karyotype (47,XX,+13 or 47,XY,+13) associated with Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13) caused by meiotic nondisjunction.
- **Differentials:** Differentiate Trisomy 13 from other trisomies like Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome - clenched hands, rocker-bottom feet) and Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome - characteristic facial features, AVSD). Also, distinguish it from deletion syndromes (like 18p-) and imprinting disorders (like Prader-Willi/Angelman).

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Turner Syndrome (45,X0) - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical presentation, genetic basis (45,X0 karyotype), and associated physiological consequences (ovarian dysgenesis, short stature, cardiovascular anomalies) of Turner syndrome, specifically linking the genetic defect to the observed phenotypes. (理解特纳综合征的临床表现、遗传基础（45,X0核型）和相关生理后果（卵巢发育不良、身材矮小、心血管异常），特别是将遗传缺陷与观察到的表型联系起来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 14-year-old female presents to her pediatrician for evaluation of delayed puberty. She reports that her breasts have not developed, and she has not yet started menstruating, unlike her peers. Her past medical history is significant for recurrent otitis media in childhood and a history of hypertension diagnosed at age 12, currently managed with lisinopril. Family history is unremarkable for premature ovarian failure or short stature. Physical examination reveals a height of 145 cm (below the 5th percentile for her age), a webbed neck, low posterior hairline, shield chest with widely spaced nipples, and bilateral cubitus valgus. A pelvic ultrasound shows streak ovaries. Karyotype analysis reveals a 45,X0 pattern in all cells examined. Serum follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) and luteinizing hormone (LH) levels are markedly elevated, while serum estradiol levels are low. An echocardiogram reveals a bicuspid aortic valve and coarctation of the aorta.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the ovarian dysgenesis observed in this patient?

(A) Loss-of-function mutation in the *FOXL2* gene, leading to impaired granulosa cell development and folliculogenesis.
(B) Deletion of the short arm (p arm) of the X chromosome, resulting in haploinsufficiency of genes critical for oocyte survival and meiosis.
(C) Gain-of-function mutation in the *SOX9* gene, causing excessive proliferation of Sertoli cells and suppression of ovarian development.
(D) Imprinting defect affecting the expression of genes on the paternal X chromosome, leading to silencing of genes required for primordial germ cell migration.
(E) Trisomy 21, resulting in overexpression of genes involved in apoptosis, leading to premature oocyte death.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 14-year-old female with delayed puberty (no breast development, no menstruation), short stature (145 cm, <5th percentile), webbed neck, low posterior hairline, shield chest, cubitus valgus. These are classic phenotypic features of Turner syndrome.
*   **Past Medical History:** Recurrent otitis media, hypertension. These are common associated conditions in Turner syndrome.
*   **Pelvic Ultrasound:** Streak ovaries. This indicates ovarian dysgenesis, a hallmark of Turner syndrome.
*   **Karyotype:** 45,X0. This confirms the diagnosis of Turner syndrome, characterized by the complete or partial absence of one X chromosome in females.
*   **Hormone Levels:** Elevated FSH and LH, low estradiol. This pattern indicates hypergonadotropic hypogonadism, consistent with primary ovarian failure due to lack of negative feedback from ovarian hormones.
*   **Echocardiogram:** Bicuspid aortic valve, coarctation of the aorta. These are common congenital heart defects associated with Turner syndrome.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining ovarian dysgenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Turner Syndrome Genetics:** The most common karyotype is 45,X0, resulting from nondisjunction during meiosis in either the mother or father, leading to the loss of the X chromosome. Mosaicism (e.g., 45,X/46,XX) is also common.
*   **Ovarian Development:** Normal ovarian development requires the presence of two X chromosomes, although one X chromosome can be inactivated. The X chromosome carries numerous genes essential for gonadal development, including genes involved in oogenesis, folliculogenesis, and germ cell survival.
*   **Mechanism of Ovarian Dysgenesis:** In 45,X0 Turner syndrome, the absence of the second X chromosome leads to haploinsufficiency for several genes located on the X chromosome. These genes are crucial for the proper development and maintenance of the ovaries. Specifically, genes on the short arm (p arm) of the X chromosome, such as *DAX1* (dosage-sensitive sex reversal, adrenal hypoplasia congenital critical region on the X chromosome, gene 1), *NR0B1* (nuclear receptor subfamily 0, group B, member 1), *SOX9* (SRY-box transcription factor 9), and *USP8* (ubiquitin specific peptidase 8), are implicated. Haploinsufficiency of these genes disrupts the normal differentiation of primordial germ cells into oocytes and the subsequent development of ovarian follicles. The absence of a second X chromosome means that the dosage of these critical genes is halved, which is insufficient for normal ovarian function. This leads to accelerated apoptosis of oocytes, resulting in streak ovaries and premature ovarian failure.
*   **Option (B) Explanation:** This option correctly identifies the deletion of the short arm (p arm) of the X chromosome and the resulting haploinsufficiency of critical genes as the primary mechanism for ovarian dysgenesis in Turner syndrome. Genes like *DAX1* and *NR0B1* on Xp are known to be dosage-sensitive and play crucial roles in ovarian development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Loss-of-function mutation in the *FOXL2* gene, leading to impaired granulosa cell development and folliculogenesis.** *FOXL2* mutations cause Bleeding Ovarian Cysts and Gonadal Dysgenesis (BOCGD) syndrome, which is characterized by ovarian failure, but it is an autosomal dominant condition and not specifically linked to the 45,X0 karyotype. While granulosa cell dysfunction is part of ovarian failure, the primary defect in Turner syndrome is the haploinsufficiency of multiple X-linked genes due to the missing chromosome.
*   **(C) Gain-of-function mutation in the *SOX9* gene, causing excessive proliferation of Sertoli cells and suppression of ovarian development.** *SOX9* is involved in sex determination. Gain-of-function mutations in *SOX9* can lead to sex reversal (XX individuals developing as males) by promoting testicular development. While *SOX9* is located on the Xp arm and haploinsufficiency contributes to Turner syndrome, a *gain*-of-function mutation would have the opposite effect, promoting male development, not ovarian dysgenesis.
*   **(D) Imprinting defect affecting the expression of genes on the paternal X chromosome, leading to silencing of genes required for primordial germ cell migration.** Genomic imprinting involves differential expression of genes depending on their parental origin. While imprinting affects development, the primary mechanism of ovarian failure in Turner syndrome is the loss of dosage of X-linked genes, not a specific imprinting defect on the paternal X chromosome. Furthermore, primordial germ cell migration occurs much earlier in development, and the main issue in Turner syndrome is the subsequent development and survival of oocytes within the gonad.
*   **(E) Trisomy 21, resulting in overexpression of genes involved in apoptosis, leading to premature oocyte death.** Trisomy 21 is Down syndrome, characterized by an extra chromosome 21. While individuals with Down syndrome can have reproductive issues, it is not the cause of Turner syndrome, and the mechanism described (overexpression of apoptotic genes) is not the primary explanation for ovarian dysgenesis in Turner syndrome. Turner syndrome is caused by monosomy X (45,X0), not trisomy 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Turner syndrome (45,X0) results from the absence of one X chromosome, leading to haploinsufficiency of critical genes on the X chromosome, particularly those on the short arm (Xp), which are essential for ovarian development and oocyte survival. This causes streak ovaries and primary ovarian failure. Key differentials for short stature and delayed puberty include constitutional delay, hypothalamic amenorrhea, hyperprolactinemia, and other genetic syndromes (e.g., Noonan syndrome, SHOX deficiency). However, the specific combination of features (short stature, webbed neck, shield chest, streak ovaries, 45,X0 karyotype) is diagnostic for Turner syndrome.
